y^{\sharp}
respectively, and then
c:\sharp(x=y)
by
p^{\sharp}
for
p:x=y
, so that the goal becomes
(\mathsf{ap}_{(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}}(p))_{*}((\mathsf{refl}_{x})^{\sharp})=p^{\sharp}
.
Finally, an Id-induction on
p
completes the proof of equivalence.

To see that (
3.1
) commutes, we do Id-induction on
p
again, then compute

\mathsf{encode}(x^{\sharp},x^{\sharp},\mathsf{ap}_{\sharp}(\mathsf{refl}_{x}))\equiv\mathsf{encode}(x^{\sharp},x^{\sharp},\mathsf{refl}_{x^{\sharp}})\equiv(\mathsf{refl}_{x^{\sharp}})_{*}(\mathsf{r}(x^{\sharp}))\equiv\mathsf{r}(x^{\sharp})=\mathsf{refl}_{x}{}^{\sharp}.\qed

We remark in passing that this implies that
\sharp
is “functorial on homotopies” as well.
Given
f,g:A\to B
and
H:f\sim g
, i.e.
H:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}f(x)=g(x)
, for any
u:\sharp A
we have
H(u_{\sharp})^{\sharp}:\sharp(f(u_{\sharp})=g(u_{\sharp}))
, which by
3.7
can be decoded to give
f(u_{\sharp})^{\sharp}=g(u_{\sharp})^{\sharp}
, i.e.
\sharp f(u)=\sharp g(u)
.
We could also go on to construct higher-dimensional aspects of a “coherent
\infty
-functor” structure on
\sharp
.

Returning to left-exactness of
\sharp
, this is actually implied formally by the conclusion of
3.7

(see
Rijke et al. 2016
;
HoTT Project 2015
)
.
However, using the special properties of
\sharp
we can give a more direct proof, beginning with a sense in which
\sharp
“preserves
\sum
s”.

Lemma 3.8
.

For any
A:\mathsf{Type}
and
B:A\to\mathsf{Type}
, we have

\sharp\left(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)\right)\;\simeq\;\mathchoice{\sum_{u:\sharp A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}\sharp B(u_{\sharp}).

(The right-hand side makes sense because inside
\sharp(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})
we may use
u
as crisp.)

Proof.

The right-hand side is codiscrete, so we can construct a map from left to right by
\sharp
-induction, sending
(a,b)
to
(a^{\sharp},b^{\sharp})
.
(Note that if
u
is
a^{\sharp}
, then
\sharp B(a^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp})\equiv\sharp B(a)
, so this is well-typed.)
In the other direction it suffices to construct an element of
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)
supposing crisp hypotheses
u::\sharp A
and
v::\sharp B(u_{\sharp})
;
but then we have
(u_{\sharp},v_{\sharp}):\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)
.
Finally, since both sides are codiscrete, both round-trip composites can be compared to identities with further
\sharp
-inductions.
∎

Theorem 3.9
.

\sharp
preserves pullbacks.

Proof.

Given
f:A\to C
and
g:B\to C
, recall that the pullback
A\times_{C}B
is
\mathchoice{\sum_{(x:A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}\mathchoice{\sum_{(y:B)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}}(f(x)=g(y))
.
By
section

3
, we have

\sharp(A\times_{C}B)\;\simeq\;\mathchoice{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}\mathchoice{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}\sharp(f(u_{\sharp})=g(v_{\sharp})).

while

\sharp A\times_{\sharp C}\sharp B\;\simeq\;\mathchoice{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(u:\sharp A)}}}\mathchoice{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}{\sum_{(v:\sharp B)}}}(\sharp f(u)=\sharp g(v))

Thus, it will suffice to prove that for all
u:\sharp A
and
v:\sharp B
we have

\sharp(f(u_{\sharp})=g(v_{\sharp}))\;\simeq\;(\sharp f(u)=\sharp g(v)).

But by definition of the functoriality of
\sharp
, the right-hand side is
f(u_{\sharp})^{\sharp}=g(v_{\sharp})^{\sharp}
.
Thus the result follows from
3.7
.
∎

Left exact monadic modalities also have many nice features, for most of which we refer to
Rijke et al. 2016
(and which are formalized in
HoTT Project 2015
).
One particularly important one is the following:

Corollary 3.10
.

\sharp
preserves
n
-types for all
n
.

Proof.

We induct on
n
.
The base case
n=-2
(i.e.
\sharp\mathbf{1}\simeq\mathbf{1}
) is true for any monadic modality.
Thus, suppose
\sharp
preserves
n
-types for some
n
, let
A
be an
(n+1)
-type, and let
u,v:\sharp A
.
Then
(u=v)
is codiscrete, hence so is the proposition “
(u=v)
is an
n
-type”, so we can use
\sharp
-induction on
u
and
v
.
But by
3.7
, for
x,y:A
we have
(x^{\sharp}=y^{\sharp})\simeq\sharp(x=y)
, which is an
n
-type by the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Rijke et al. 2016
and
HoTT Project 2015

show that if a left-exact monadic modality is also
accessible
in a technical sense (defined there; see also
section

9
), then the universe of modal types is modal.
We can prove this for
\sharp
without accessibility, because of our formation rule for
\sharp A
.
The marker {
UA
} means that we need the full univalence axiom (although of course for the subuniverse of propositions we would need only propositional extensionality).

Theorem
{UA}
3.11
.

The universe
\mathsf{Codisc}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}\mathsf{iscodiscrete}(A)
of codiscrete types is codiscrete.

Proof.

Suppose
X:\sharp\mathsf{Codisc}
; we want to construct an element of
\mathsf{Codisc}
(to define a retraction).
The first component of it will be
\sharp
of some type, and for the purpose of constructing that type we may assume
X
is crisp.
Thus, we have
X_{\sharp}:\mathsf{Codisc}
, which we can destruct further as
(A,c)
for
A:\mathsf{Type}
and
c:\mathsf{iscodiscrete}(A)
.
Now we use
A
to be the type required.
Explicitly, from
X:\sharp\mathsf{Codisc}
we have constructed

\sharp(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(X_{\sharp})):\mathsf{Type}

By
3.5
, this type is codiscrete, so gives an element of
\mathsf{Codisc}
.

To show that this is a retraction, if
X
is of the form
Y^{\sharp}
, then we have

\sharp(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(Y^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}))\equiv\sharp(\mathsf{pr}_{1}(Y))

which is equivalent to
\mathsf{pr}_{1}(Y)
, since by
\mathsf{pr}_{2}(Y)
the latter is codiscrete.
This completes the proof since codiscreteness is a proposition.
∎

It follows that we can interpret the formal syntax of ordinary type theory (with only one kind of context) entirely into the codiscrete types, with
\mathsf{Codisc}
playing the role of
the
universe, obtaining a model with
\prod
-types,
\sum
-types, a unit type, and identity types.
We can also obtain some (higher) inductive types in this model, at least up to homotopy
(in the sense of
UFP 2013
, §5.5)
, simply by applying
\sharp
to their ordinary versions; this includes binary sums, the empty type, the natural numbers,
n
-truncations, suspensions, and more general colimits.

This doesn’t appear to work for general W-types, however; if
W
is generated by a constructor
\mathsf{sup}:\mathchoice{\prod_{a:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}(B(a)\to W)\to W
, there isn’t even any obvious way to define a function
\mathchoice{\prod_{a:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}(B(a)\to\sharp W)\to\sharp W
.
It may be tempting to write “
{\lambda}a.\,{\lambda}b.\,\mathsf{sup}(a,{\lambda}y.\,b(y)_{\sharp})^{\sharp}
”, but this is ill-typed: although the outer
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}
makes all variables in the
current
context crisp, the inner
\lambda y
binds a
new
,
cohesive
variable
y
, so that
b(y)
is not crisp and we cannot apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\sharp}
to it.

We could work around this if
\sharp
were accessible, by adding the appropriate localization constructors to any HIT.
Accessibility is a perfectly reasonable axiom, since it holds in all known examples.
But in this paper we have no need for it, and moreover we want the base spatial type theory of this part (as opposed to the real-cohesive extension of it in the next part) to be free of axioms.

As an example of translating type theory into the codiscrete universe, let us define the Dedekind real numbers codiscretely.
Since the types
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Q}
are abstractly equivalent to
\mathbb{N}
, and
\sharp\mathbb{N}
is the natural numbers in the codiscrete world, it follows that the codiscrete versions of
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Q}
will be equivalent to
\sharp\mathbb{Z}
and
\sharp\mathbb{Q}
respectively.
Now if we expand out the usual definition of the Dedekind reals
\mathbb{R}
into the basic type constructors, we obtain the type shown first in
fig.

4
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{Prop})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{Prop})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{Prop})}}\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\leftrightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}(q<r)\times L(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\leftrightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}U(q)\times(q<r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}(L(q)\times U(q))\to\emptyset\right)\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}(q<r)\rightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|L(q)+U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}}{\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(q<^{\prime}r)\times L(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U(q)\times(q<^{\prime}r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(L(q)\times U(q))\to\sharp\emptyset\right)\land\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(q<^{\prime}r)\rightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\sharp(L(q)+U(r))\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

Figure 4.
The Dedekind reals and their codiscrete version

Thus, their codiscrete version should be the type
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
shown second in
fig.

4
.
In contrast to
\mathbb{Z}
and
\mathbb{Q}
, it is not at all obvious that
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
has anything to do with
\sharp\mathbb{R}
; in the former all intermediate constructions are discontinuous, whereas in the latter we do everything continuously and then at the very end forget the resulting topology.

We will see later on that they
are
in fact equivalent (
6.34
), but this is a very special property of
\mathbb{R}
.
In most cases, the “codiscrete version” of a type
A
is very different from
\sharp A
.
For instance,
\sharp(\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R})
should be the set of
continuous
functions
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
topologized codiscretely, whereas
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
is the set of
discontinuous
functions.

Remark 3.12
.

The overall conclusion of this section so far can be summarized as “
\sharp
is a left-exact monadic modality” (plus
3.11
, which could be obtained from accessibility).
This is a purely cohesive statement, i.e. it can be stated without any need for crisp variables.
In
Schreiber & Shulman 2012
, using ordinary type theory without crispness, we took this statement as the axiomatic definition of
\sharp
.
The benefit of instead deriving it from rules involving crisp variables is that, as we will see in
section

4
,
\sharp
then automatically has the correct relationship to
\flat
.

3.1.
Excluded middle revisited

With
\sharp
in hand, we can reformulate the crisp law of excluded middle (
Axiom LEM
) as a single term (modulo universe polymorphism).

Lemma
{LEM}
3.13

(The sharp law of excluded middle)
.

\mathchoice{\prod_{P:\mathsf{Prop}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}\sharp(P\vee\neg P)
.

Proof.

Assume
P:\mathsf{Prop}
.
By the introduction rule for
\sharp
, it suffices to prove
P\vee\neg P
assuming a crisp
P::\mathsf{Prop}
.
But this is exactly
Axiom LEM
.
∎

Let us now return to the topological viewpoint, where codiscrete types have the codiscrete topology.
More generally, if
P:A\to\mathsf{Type}
and each
P(x)
is codiscrete, then
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x)
must have the “final topology” induced by the first projection, i.e. the coarsest possible topology such that the first projection is continuous.
To see this, note that sections
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(x)
are equivalent to sections
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)
, and hence to conclusions
f(x):P(x)
under crisp hypothesis
x::A
; but by our topological gloss, these are supposed to be simply discontinuous sections.
Thus, the sections
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(x)
take no account of any topology on
P
, so it must be that
P
has the final topology.

Remark 3.14
.

This topological gloss explains why the universe of codiscrete types must itself be codiscrete.
A map into
A
whose domain has the final topology is uniquely specified by a map into the underlying set (or
\infty
-groupoid) of
A
, i.e. it takes no account of the topology of
A
.
Thus, a family of codiscrete types over
A
should be the same as such a family over
\sharp A
, which is to say that any map
P:A\to\mathsf{Codisc}
must factor through
\sharp A
; thus
\mathsf{Codisc}
must be codiscrete.

In particular, to say that a predicate
P:A\to\mathsf{Prop}
is pointwise codiscrete must mean that it is a
subspace
, with the induced topology.
We have already seen in
section

2.1
a different type-theoretic property that ought also to characterize subspaces, namely that
P
is
\neg\neg
-closed.
It turns out that one simple assumption beyond
Axiom LEM
guarantees that these two properties coincide.

(See also
Lawvere & Menni 2015
, Corollary 4.5.)

Axiom
\sharp\emptyset
.

\emptyset
is codiscrete.

Theorem
{LEM,
\sharp\emptyset
}
3.15
.

(1)

A proposition
P
is codiscrete if and only if
\neg\neg P\to P
.

(2)

For any proposition
P
we have
\sharp P\simeq\neg\neg P
.

(3)

For any type
A
we have
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}\simeq\neg\neg A
.

Proof.

We prove
(1)
first.
Since
P\to\neg\neg P
always, we have
\neg\neg P\to P
if and only if
\neg\neg P=P
.
Now since
\emptyset
is codiscrete, so is
\neg\neg P
for any
P
. Thus if
\neg\neg P=P
, then
P
is also codiscrete.

Conversely, suppose
P
is codiscrete, and suppose that
\neg\neg P
; we must show
P
.
By crisp excluded middle, we have
\sharp(P\vee\neg P)
.
Since our goal
P
is codiscrete, by
\sharp
-induction we have
P\vee\neg P
.
But since
\neg\neg P
, it must be that
P
.
(Note that each of the two assumptions
Axiom LEM
and
Axiom

⁢
♯
∅

is used in exactly one direction of this equivalence.)

Now
(2)
follows because
\sharp
and
\neg\neg
both reflect a proposition into the sub-poset of codiscrete propositions.
Likewise,
(3)
follows because
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\right\|\mathclose{}
and
\neg\neg
both reflect an arbitrary type into that same poset (since
\neg\neg A
is always a proposition).
∎

In particular, in this case the statement of
section

3.1
is equivalent to

\mathchoice{\prod_{P:\mathsf{Prop}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}\neg\neg(P\vee\neg P)

which is a provable statement in type theory.
Of course, we used
Axiom LEM
in proving
3.15
, so this doesn’t in any way absolve us from having to assume
Axiom LEM
.
But it is nice to see that the cohesive version of excluded middle implied by our
Axiom LEM
is just the one that is already provable in constructive type theory.

3.2.
The axiom of choice

We can now revisit the axiom of choice.
First, however, let us consider all the ways of saying that a family
P:A\to\mathsf{Type}
is “inhabited”, of which we now have
four
.
Here is a list of them with their intended topological meanings.

(1)

\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(x)
says that
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x))\to A
has a continuous section.

(2)

\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)
says that this projection has a
discontinuous
section.

(3)

\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
says that this projection is a quotient map.

(4)

\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
says that this projection is surjective on points.

(Assuming
Axiom LEM
, this is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\neg\neg{P(x)}
.)

These topological glosses are mainly for our intuition, but in some cases we can prove precise versions of them in type theory.
For instance, statement
(1)
makes sense internally and is obviously true.
Regarding
(2)
, we can prove:

Lemma 3.16
.

\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)
if and only if
\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\sharp(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x))\to\sharp A
has a section.

Proof.

By
\sharp
-induction
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)
is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\sharp A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}\sharp P(x_{\sharp})
.
This, in turn, is equivalent to saying that
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\sharp A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}\sharp P(x_{\sharp}))\to\sharp A
has a section.
But by
section

3
,
\sharp(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x))\simeq\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\sharp A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\sum_{(x:\sharp A)}}}\sharp P(x_{\sharp})
, and it is easy to see that the projections match up.
∎

As for
(3)
, if
A
and
P
are sets we can consider it justified by
UFP 2013
, Theorem 10.1.5
(surjections of sets are regular epimorphisms).
The general case requires a more refined notion of “quotient map”, but is also true when suitably formulated
(see
Kraus 2016
;
van Doorn 2016
;
Rijke 2017
)
.

Finally, regarding
(4)
we can say the following.

Lemma 3.17
.

\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
if and only if
\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\sharp(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x))\to\sharp A
is “codiscretely surjective”, i.e. for all
x:\sharp A
we have
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Note that “codiscretely surjective” is what we get by interpreting “surjective” in the universe of codiscrete types, as discussed at the end of
section

3
.
Thus, it is the appropriate way to say that a map of codiscrete types “is surjective” in the untopologized world.

Proof.

By
\sharp
-induction
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\sharp A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}{\prod_{(x:\sharp A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Thus, it will suffice to show that for any
x:\sharp A
we have
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Suppose first that
q:\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
; we will show
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.
We apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}
, making our goal
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
and our hypotheses crisp, allowing us to form
q_{\sharp}:\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Now by functoriality of
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\right\|\mathclose{}
, it suffices to assume
p:P(x_{\sharp})
and prove
\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)
.
But then
(x_{\sharp},p)^{\sharp}:\sharp(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x))
, and

\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}((x_{\sharp},p)^{\sharp})\equiv\mathsf{pr}_{1}(x_{\sharp},p)^{\sharp}\equiv x_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}\equiv x.

Next suppose that
z:\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
; we will show
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Since
\sharp
takes values in codiscrete types, it suffices to show
\sharp\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Thus, we can apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}
to make our hypotheses crisp while keeping the goal
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Now we can form
z_{\sharp}:\mathopen{}\left\|\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}}(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
, and since
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
is a proposition we can destruct
z_{\sharp}
to obtain
q:\sharp(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x))
and
\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}(q)=x
.
Next, by
\sharp
-induction we can assume
q
is
(y,p)^{\sharp}
for some
y:A
and
p:P(y)
.
But
\sharp\mathsf{pr}_{1}((y,p)^{\sharp})\equiv\mathsf{pr}_{1}(y,p)^{\sharp}\equiv y^{\sharp}
, so we have
y^{\sharp}=x
.
And since
x
is crisp we have
x\equiv x_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}
, so
y^{\sharp}=x_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}
.
But by
3.7
this type is equivalent to
\sharp(y=x_{\sharp})
.
Thus, with another
\sharp
-induction we obtain
s:y=x_{\sharp}
, so we can transport
p
along
s
to get an element of
P(x_{\sharp})
, and finally map it into
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x_{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}
.
∎

Corollary 3.18
.

If
f:A\to B
is an embedding and
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}\sharp\mathsf{fib}_{f}(x)
, then
\sharp f
is an equivalence.

Proof.

Since
f
is an embedding, so is
\sharp f
.
Moreover,
\sharp f
has codiscrete fibers.
Thus,
section

3.2
is sufficient to ensure that it is an equivalence.
∎

Now, since we cannot expect every surjection to have a continuous section, the conclusion of our axiom of choice should be
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)
.
Therefore, it ought to suffice as a hypothesis that it be surjective on points, i.e.
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Of course, the conclusion must be further
(-1)
-truncated outside the
\prod
, to be consistent with univalence.
This might lead us to state the axiom of choice as

(3.2)

\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)\to\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}.

However, I believe this is not yet quite right either.
Remember that the entire statement is also parametrized by
A
and
P
.
Thus, (
3.2
) is actually asserting that one subobject of
\mathchoice{\sum_{A:\mathsf{Type}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}(A\to\mathsf{Type})
is contained in another.
But the first subobject is codiscrete — i.e. has the subspace topology — whereas the second is
not
, at least not by definition:
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\right\|\mathclose{}
doesn’t preserve codiscreteness.
Moreover, the reverse implication always holds; thus (
3.2
) is actually asserting that these two subobjects agree, and hence that the one on the right is a subspace even though we haven’t forced it to be.
This seems unreasonable (although I do not currently have a concrete counterexample).
Thus, instead we take the following:

Axiom AC
.

(Sharp axiom of choice)
For any set
A
and type family
P:A\to\mathsf{Type}
, we have

(3.3)

\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)\to\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}.

This is admittedly quite a mouthful, and one is left wondering whether it is good for anything.
Are there simpler special cases?
What about, for instance, the
countable
axiom of choice, where
A=\mathbb{N}
; surely there shouldn’t be any topology on
\mathbb{N}
to get in the way.
Thus, we now turn to making sense of what it means to have a
discrete
topology.

4.
The
\flat
modality

4.1.
A no-go theorem

Discreteness is encoded by the modality
\flat
that is dual to
\sharp
.
Whereas
\sharp
is a reflector into the subcategory of codiscrete types,
\flat
is a coreflector into the subcategory of discrete types.
However, unlike
\sharp
, the behavior of
\flat
cannot be stated as ordinary type-theoretic rules or axioms, because the resulting pullback-stability would cause it to degenerate.
This is the conclusion of the following “no-go theorem”.

Theorem 4.1
.

Suppose we have the following data:

(1)

A predicate
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}:\mathsf{Type}\to\mathsf{Prop}
that is invariant under equivalence, i.e.
(A\simeq B)\to\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(A)\to\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(B)
.
(This condition is, of course, automatic with univalence.)

(2)

An operation
\Box:\mathsf{Type}\to\mathsf{Type}
, such that
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(\Box(A))
for all
A
.

(3)

For each
A:\mathsf{Type}
, a function
\varepsilon_{A}:\Box A\to A
.

(4)

If
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(B)
, then postcomposition with
\varepsilon_{A}
is an equivalence
(B\to\Box A)\simeq(B\to A)
.

Then there exists
U:\mathsf{Prop}
such that for all
A
we have

(a)

\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(A)\leftrightarrow(A\to U)
and

(b)

\Box A\simeq(A\times U)

Proof.

Firstly, we observe that if
A
is a proposition, so is
\Box A
.
For if
x,y:\Box A
, then we have two functions
\mathsf{const}_{x},\mathsf{const}_{y}:\Box A\to\Box A
whose composites with
\varepsilon_{A}
are equal.
Hence
\mathsf{const}_{x}=\mathsf{const}_{y}
and thus (evaluating them at
x
, say)
x=y
.

Secondly, we observe that if
\varepsilon_{A}:\Box A\to A
has a section
s:A\to\Box A
, then
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(A)
.
It suffices to show that
s\circ\varepsilon_{A}=\mathsf{id}_{\Box A}
; but these are again two maps
\Box A\to\Box A
having the same composite with
\varepsilon_{A}
.

Thirdly, we can extend
\Box
to a functor such that
\varepsilon
is a natural transformation from
\Box
to the identity.
(This is also the dual of facts we observed in
section

3
for
\sharp
.)

Now let
U\vcentcolon\equiv\Box\mathbf{1}
.
First we prove
(a)
.
On one hand, if
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(A)
, then
A\to\mathbf{1}
factors through
U
.
On the other hand, suppose
f:A\to U
; we will construct a section
s:A\to\Box A
.
For any
x:A
we have
\mathsf{const}_{x}:\mathbf{1}\to A
, and hence by functoriality
\Box\mathsf{const}_{x}:\Box\mathbf{1}\to\Box A
.
But
f:A\to\Box\mathbf{1}
, so we have
\Box\mathsf{const}_{x}(f(x)):\Box A
; we define this to be
s(x)
.
Now by naturality of
\varepsilon
, we have

\varepsilon_{A}(s(x))=\varepsilon_{A}(\Box\mathsf{const}_{x}(f(x)))=\mathsf{const}_{x}(\varepsilon_{\mathbf{1}}(f(x)))=x.

Thus,
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(A)
; this completes the proof of
(a)
.

Now we prove
(b)
.
By
(a)
, we have
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(A\times U)
; thus the projection
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:A\times U\to A
factors through
\varepsilon_{A}
by some
g:A\times U\to\Box A
.
On the other hand, since
\mathsf{in}_{\Box}(\Box A)
we have
f:\Box A\to U
, and thus
(\varepsilon_{A},f):\Box A\to A\times U
.
The composite in one direction is

(\varepsilon_{A},f)\circ g=(\varepsilon_{A}\circ g,f\circ g)=(\mathsf{pr}_{1},\mathsf{pr}_{2})=\mathsf{id}_{A\times U}

where
\varepsilon_{A}\circ g=\mathsf{id}_{A}
by definition of
g
, while
f\circ g=\mathsf{id}_{U}
since
U
is a proposition.
The composite in the other direction is a map
\Box A\to\Box A
whose composite with
\varepsilon_{A}
is
\varepsilon_{A}
, hence it must be the identity.
This shows
(b)
.
∎

We certainly don’t expect
\flat
to be of the form
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\times U)
, so this means that we cannot describe
\flat
as ordinary type-theoretic rules or axioms.
The central point of
4.1
is that the universal property
(4)
can be applied in an arbitrary context; this is what enables us to construct a section
A\to\Box A
from a map
A\to\Box\mathbf{1}
.
Thus, in order to describe a more general coreflection, we need to restrict the context in which it and its universal property can be applied.
In our current setup, an obvious restriction to try is that the context should include only crisp variables.
This is what we will do.

4.2.
The rules for
\flat

We now describe our rules for
\flat
in English; a type-theoretic presentation is shown in
fig.

5
.

•

For any
crisp
type
A::\mathsf{Type}
, there is a type
\flat A
.

•

For any
crisp

a::A
, we have
a^{\flat}:\flat A
.

•

An element of
\flat A
may always be assumed to be of the form
u^{\flat}
for some crisp variable
u::A
.
This is called
\flat
-induction
.
Syntactically, if
C
is a type depending on
x:\flat A
, and
N:C[u^{\flat}/x]
is an expression depending on a crisp variable
u::A
, while we have an element
M:\flat A
, we have an induced element

(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N):C[M/x].

Here the notation
C[M/x]
indicates that the expression
M
is substituted for the variable
x
in
C
.

UFP 2013
used function notation when discussing induction principles, but for
\flat
we cannot since we do not have a basic notion of “function of a crisp variable” (indeed, one of the purposes of
\flat
is to provide such a notion); thus we have to talk about substituting into expressions rather than evaluating functions.

•

The expected computation rule:
(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\equiv N[M/u]

if
M::A
is crisp.

It is crucial that we cannot apply
\flat
to types containing cohesive variables.
This reflects the fact that, by
4.1
, “retopologizing discretely” is not a
continuous
operation on the space of spaces.
(It
is
possible to do this for “retopologizing codiscretely”, so that
\sharp
requires no such restriction.
Indeed, by
3.11
the type of codiscrete types is codiscrete, so any function into it is automatically continuous.)

\frac{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash A:\mathsf{Type}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\flat A:\mathsf{Type}}\frac{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:A}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash M^{\flat}:\flat A}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma,x:\flat A\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash M:\flat A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\Gamma\vdash N:C[u^{\flat}/x]}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N):C[M/x]}\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma,x:\flat A\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\Gamma\vdash N:C[u^{\flat}/x]}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\equiv N[M/u]\;:\>C[M^{\flat}/x]}

Figure 5.
Rules for
\flat

Remark 4.2
.

Strictly speaking, the syntax “
\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N
” should also notate the type family
C
, since that is not generally uniquely inferrable.
However, when working informally this will not matter much.

Remark 4.3
.

Dually to
\sharp
(see
section

3
),
\flat
is a
positive
type former.
9
9

9

Well, at least in the usual sense that this adjective is used for dependent type theories.
In the sequent calculus of
Licata & Shulman 2016
,
\flat
is (conjecturally) positive in the precise sense of “focusing”, but our presentation of
\flat
breaks some of this positivity.
However, this often happens to positive types when translating from sequent calculus into dependent type theory.

This means, roughly, that its elimination rule is chosen to match its introduction rule(s).
For instance, the coproduct
A+B
is positive because to define a (dependent) function
out
of it, we use case analysis, which essentially means we have to specify the values of that function on all the ways to construct an element of
A+B
(namely via
\mathsf{inl}
or
\mathsf{inr}
).
In the case of
\flat
, the introduction rule says that the way to get an element of
\flat A
is to have a crisp element of
A
; thus the elimination rule says that to define a function out of
\flat A
it suffices to assume we have a crisp element of
A
.

In contrast to negative type formers, whose uniqueness principles tend to be judgmental rules, positive type formers tend to have uniqueness principles that are only typal, but can be proven from their elimination and computation rules.
As we will now see, this is also the case for
\flat
.

Lemma 4.4

(Uniqueness principle for
\flat
)
.

Let
A::\mathsf{Type}
,
C:\flat A\to\mathsf{Type}
, and
f:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}C(x)
.
For any
x:\flat A
we have

(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat}))=f(x).

Proof.

Let
P(x)\vcentcolon\equiv((\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat}))=f(x))
; then
P:\flat A\to\mathsf{Type}
.
Thus, in proving
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}P(x)
we can use
\flat
-induction.
But when
x
is
v^{\flat}
, we have
P(v^{\flat})\equiv(f(v^{\flat})=f(v^{\flat}))
, using the computation rule for
\flat
on the left-hand side, and this is trivial by reflexivity.
In symbols, we have

(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{refl}_{f(v^{\flat})})\;:\;(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat}))=f(x).\qed

As with
\sharp
, we can make
\flat
into a functor, at least on crisp functions: given
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
and
f::A\to B
, we define
\flat f:\flat A\to\flat B
by

(4.1)

\flat f(x)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u)^{\flat}).

We might be tempted to write instead
(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u))^{\flat}
, but this is invalid because the inside of
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
cannot contain cohesive variables such as
x
.
For the same reason, we require not only
A
and
B
, but also
f
, to be crisp.
In particular, we cannot deduce from this a map
(A\to B)\to(\flat A\to\flat B)
.
The best we can do is a map
\flat(A\to B)\to\flat(\flat A\to\flat B)
, defined by
\flat
-induction followed by (
4.1
).

When we try to verify the functoriality of this operation, we see that we need some additional lemmas.
Given
A,B,C::\mathsf{Type}
and
f::A\to B
and
g::B\to C
, in addition to (
4.1
) we have

(4.2)

\flat g(y)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;g(v)^{\flat})

and thus for any
x:\flat A
, we have

\flat g(\flat f(x))\equiv\big(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u)^{\flat})\;\mathsf{in}\;g(v)^{\flat}\big)

but on the right-hand side, what are we to do with this “nested
\mathsf{let}
”?
We have to apply the following lemma, called a
(typal) commuting conversion
.

Lemma 4.5

(Commuting
let
with itself)
.

Whenever both sides typecheck, we have

\big(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\;\mathsf{in}\;P\big)=\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq N\;\mathsf{in}\;P)\big).

Moreover, if the left-hand side typechecks, so does the right-hand side, while if the right-hand side typechecks and
u
does not occur in
P
, then the left-hand side typechecks.

Proof.

One proof is to do
\flat
-induction on
M
:
when
M
is
w^{\flat}
for
w::A
, both sides of the goal reduce to
(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq N[w/u]\;\mathsf{in}\;P)
, so we can apply reflexivity.
Another is to apply the uniqueness principle in reverse, followed by the computation rule:

\displaystyle\hskip 79.6678pt\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\;\mathsf{in}\;P

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;w^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq w^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\;\mathsf{in}\;P)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;w^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq N[w/u]\;\mathsf{in}\;P)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq N\;\mathsf{in}\;P)

The second proof makes the typechecking conditions clearer, as these rewriting steps can always be performed forwards, whereas to perform them backwards we need to know that
u
does not occur in
P
.
∎

Remark 4.6
.

In
section

2.1
we mentioned the difference between implication and entailment, whose type-theoretic syntaxes are
\Gamma\vdash f:A\to B
and
\Gamma,x:A\vdash f(x):B
respectively.
Both of these, however, are single “hypothetical judgments”, whose hypotheses consist of a context of variables (either “
\Gamma
” or “
\Gamma,x:A
”).
By contrast,
section

4.2
cannot be expressed as a
single
hypothetical judgment, because
M
,
N
, and
P
must be “metavariables” denoting
expressions
rather than ordinary variables.
This should be clear from the condition “
u
does not occur in
P
”, which would be nonsensical if
P
were a variable.
In type-theoretic language,
section

4.2
is a
derivable rule
rather than a single judgment, because among its
hypotheses
are other
hypothetical judgments
;
fig.

6
shows it written out in type-theoretic syntax.
We will try to avoid such “meta-statements” as much as possible, but in this section and the next we will need a few more of them.

\frac{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash A:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash B:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma,y:\flat B\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash M:\flat A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\Gamma\vdash N:\flat B\quad\Delta,v::B\mid\Gamma\vdash P:C[v^{\flat}/y]}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\big(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\;\mathsf{in}\;P\big)=\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq N\;\mathsf{in}\;P)\big)}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:commute-let})}\frac{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash A:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma,x:\flat A\vdash B:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma,x:\flat A,y:B\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:\flat A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\Gamma\vdash N:B[u^{\flat}/x]\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash f:\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}C}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash f\big(M,(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\big)=\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat},N)\big)}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:commute-app})}

Figure 6.
Commuting conversions

With
section

4.2
, we can now verify the functoriality of
\flat
:

\displaystyle\flat g(\flat f(x))

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u)^{\flat})\;\mathsf{in}\;g(v)^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq f(u)^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;g(v)^{\flat})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;g(f(u))^{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv\flat(g\circ f)(x).

Functoriality on identities is easier, using the uniqueness principle:

\flat(\mathsf{id}_{A})(x)\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat})=x

Note that unlike the functoriality of
\sharp
, these are typal rather than judgmental equalities.
Thus, they should actually be viewed as the first level of a “coherent
\infty
-functor”; later on we will construct the next level by showing that
\flat
is also “functorial on homotopies”.

Continuing with the analogy to
\sharp
, in place of the “unit”
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}:A\to\sharp A
we have a “counit”
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A\to A
, which is defined for any (crisp!)
A::\mathsf{Type}
by

x_{\flat}\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u).

The computation rule tells us that for
v::A
we have

(4.3)

v^{\flat}{}_{\flat}\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq v^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;u)\equiv v.

We expect
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}
to be a natural transformation (and eventually a “coherent
\infty
-natural transformation”) from
\flat
to the identity functor.
To verify this, let
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
and
f::A\to B
and fix any
x:\flat A
; then we would like to compute

\displaystyle(\flat f(x))_{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u)^{\flat})_{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u)^{\flat}{}_{\flat}

(
section

4.2
)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u)

\displaystyle=f(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u)

(?)

\displaystyle\equiv f(x_{\flat}).

To fill in the step marked ?, we need another commuting conversion.

Lemma 4.7

(Commuting
let
with function application)
.

Suppose given types
A
,
B
, and
C
, where
A
is crisp,
B
may depend on
x:\flat A
, and
C
may depend on
x:\flat A
and
y:B
.
Also suppose we have expressions
M:\flat A
and
N:B[u^{\flat}/x]
and
f:\mathchoice{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(y:B)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}}C
, where
N
may depend on
u::A
.
Then we may conclude

f\big(M,(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\big)=\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat},N)\big).

Like
section

4.2
, this is formally a “derivable rule”; in
fig.

6
we have written it out in type-theoretic syntax.

Proof.

As before,
\flat
-induction on
M
reduces both sides to
f(w^{\flat},N[w/u])
; or we can expand and contract:

\displaystyle\hskip 76.82234ptf\big(M,(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\big)

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;f\big(v^{\flat},(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq v^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\big)

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;f(v^{\flat},N[v/u])

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat},N).\qed

Analogous commuting conversions can be proven for the constructors and eliminators of all other ordinary types; we leave them to the reader.
However, there is one conversion we need that is more subtle, relating to
\flat
itself.
To see how this arises, consider the obvious dual claim to (
4.3
), that
x_{\flat}{}^{\flat}=x
.
Note that
x_{\flat}{}^{\flat}
only makes sense when
x
is a
crisp
element of
\flat A
, since we can only apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
to crisp elements.
In this case we would like to write

(4.4)

x_{\flat}{}^{\flat}\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u)^{\flat}=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat})=x,

using the uniqueness principle (
section

4.2
) at the end; but in the middle we need to be able to commute
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
past
\mathsf{let}
.
However, if we try to prove this as we did for the other commuting conversions, we run into the problem that (as remarked above)
x::\flat A
must be crisp, whereas
\flat
-induction is only stated for types
C
depending on a
cohesive
variable
x:\flat A
.
Thus, we now take a brief digression to investigate
crisp induction principles
.

5.
Crisp induction principles

Here is the lemma we need about
\flat
.
Note that its proof involves
\sharp
as well!

Lemma 5.1

(Crisp
\flat
-induction)
.

Let
C
be a crisp type depending on a crisp variable
x::\flat A
, and
N::C[u^{\flat}/x]
a crisp expression depending on a crisp variable
u::A
.
If we have a crisp element
M::\flat A
, then we have an element of
C[M/x]
, which we denote

(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\Coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N):C[M/x].

Moreover, if
M::A
then

(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\Coloneqq M^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\equiv N[M/u].

Proof.

If we have only a cohesive variable
x:\flat A
, we can still write
\sharp C:\mathsf{Type}
, since cohesive variables can become crisp inside
\sharp(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})
.
Similarly, if
u::A
we have
N^{\sharp}:\sharp C[u^{\flat}/x]
.
Thus, since
M:\flat A
, we have
(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\sharp}):\sharp C[M/x]
.
And since this expression is crisp, we have
(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\sharp})_{\sharp}:C[M/x]
, which we define to be “
\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\Coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N
”.
Finally, if
M::A
then

(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\sharp})_{\sharp}\equiv N[M/u]^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}\equiv N[M/u].\qed

This proof is somewhat tricky, so we include a derivation tree for it; see
fig.

7
.

\frac{\frac{\frac{\Delta,x::\flat A\mid\cdot\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}}{\Delta\mid x:\flat A\vdash\sharp C:\mathsf{Type}}\hskip 8.19447pt\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:\flat A\hskip 8.19447pt\frac{\Delta,u::A\mid\cdot\vdash N:C[u^{\flat}/x]}{\Delta,u::A\mid\cdot\vdash N^{\sharp}:\sharp C[u^{\flat}/x]}}{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\sharp}):\sharp C[M/x]}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\sharp})_{\sharp}:C[M/x]}

Figure 7.
A derivation tree for
section

5

The crispness restriction on
C
and
N
can be worked around in the usual “Frobenius” way: if
C
and
N
depend on some cohesive variable
y:B
, we can form
C^{\prime}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{\prod_{y:B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:B)}}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}{\prod_{(y:B)}}}C
and
N^{\prime}\vcentcolon\equiv{\lambda}y.\,N
and apply
section

5
to them instead.
By induction over metatheoretic natural numbers, we can incorporate an arbitrary cohesive context.

Now we can prove the needed commuting conversion.

Lemma 5.2

(Commuting
let
with
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
)
.

Given crisp expressions
M::\flat A
and
N::B[u^{\flat}/x]
, where
B
depends on a crisp variable
x::\flat A
and
N
depends on
u::A
, we have

(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)^{\flat}=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\flat})

Proof.

By
section

5
, we can replace
M
by
w^{\flat}
for some
w::A
.
Now both sides reduce to
N^{\flat}[w/u]
.
∎

At last, the computation (
4.4
) makes sense.
Later on we will also have use for the corresponding type-level commuting conversion:

Lemma 5.3

(Commuting
let
with
\flat
)
.

Given crisp expressions
M::\flat A
and
N::\mathsf{Type}
, where
N
may depend on
u::A
, we have

\flat(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat N).

\frac{\Delta,x::\flat A\mid\cdot\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:\flat A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\cdot\vdash N:C[u^{\flat}/x]}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\Coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N):C[M/x]}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:crisp-flat-ind})}(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\Coloneqq M^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;N)\equiv N[M/u]\frac{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash A:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta,x::\flat A\mid\cdot\vdash B:\mathsf{Type}\quad\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:\flat A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\cdot\vdash N:B[u^{\flat}/x]}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)^{\flat}=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N^{\flat})}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:commute-flat})}\frac{\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash A:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:\flat A\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\cdot\vdash N:\mathsf{Type}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\flat(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;N)=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat N)}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:commute-flat})}

Figure 8.
Crisp
\flat
-induction and commuting
let
with
\flat

The idea used to prove
section

5
can be applied to other positive types as well.
For instance, since we assert the rules of ordinary type theory only for cohesive variables, the eliminator for the coproduct type is

\frac{\Delta\mid\Gamma,z:A+B\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash M:A+B\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma,x:A\vdash c_{A}:C[\mathsf{inl}(x)/z]\quad\Delta\mid\Gamma,y:B\vdash c_{B}:C[\mathsf{inr}(y)/z]}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}(z.C,x.c_{A},y.c_{B},M):C[M/z]}

Thus, the element
M
being case-analyzed is cohesive, the variables
x
and
y
that it is destructed into are cohesive, and the goal
C
depends on a cohesive
z:A+B
.
The first restriction is not significant since we can always use a crisp
M
as a cohesive one, but the others are nontrivial.
The method of
section

5
allows us to remove them.

Theorem 5.4

(Crisp case analysis)
.

Let
A
and
B
be crisp types, let
C
be a crisp type depending on a crisp variable
z::A+B
, and let
N::C[\mathsf{inl}(u)/z]
and
P::C[\mathsf{inr}(v)/z]
be crisp expressions depending on crisp variables
u::A
and
v::B
.
If we have a crisp
M::A+B
, then we have an element
\mathsf{case}^{\flat}(z.C,u.N,v.P,M):C[M/z]
, which computes to
N
and
P
on
\mathsf{inl}
and
\mathsf{inr}
.

fig.

9
shows
5.4
in type-theoretic syntax.

Proof.

If we have only a cohesive variable
z:A+B
, we can still write
\sharp C:\mathsf{Type}
.
Similarly, if
u:A
we have
N^{\sharp}:\sharp C[\mathsf{inl}(u)/z]
, and if
v:B
we have
P^{\sharp}:\sharp C[\mathsf{inr}(v)/z]
.
Thus, treating the crisp
M::A+B
as cohesive, we can do ordinary case analysis to yield
\mathsf{case}(z.\sharp C,u.N^{\sharp},v.P^{\sharp},M):\sharp C[M/z]
.
But since
C
,
M
,
N
, and
P
are crisp, so is this expression, so we can write

\mathsf{case}(z.\sharp C,u.N^{\sharp},v.P^{\sharp},M)_{\sharp}:C[M/z].

The computation rule follows from those for
\mathsf{case}
and
\sharp
combined.
∎

\frac{\Delta,z:A+B\mid\cdot\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta,u::A\mid\cdot\vdash N:C[\mathsf{inl}(u)/z]\quad\Delta,v::B\mid\cdot\vdash P:C[\mathsf{inr}(v)/z]\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash M:A+B}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{case}^{\flat}(z.C,u.N,v.P,M):C[M/z]}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:crisp-case})}\mathsf{case}^{\flat}(z.C,u.N,v.P,\mathsf{inl}(u))\equiv N\mathsf{case}^{\flat}(z.C,u.N,v.P,\mathsf{inr}(v))\equiv P\frac{\Delta,u::B,v::B,p::u=v\mid\cdot\vdash C:\mathsf{Type}\quad\Delta,u::B\mid\cdot\vdash d:C[u/v,\mathsf{refl}_{u}/p]\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash b_{1}:B\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash b_{2}:B\quad\Delta\mid\cdot\vdash q:b_{1}=b_{2}}{\Delta\mid\Gamma\vdash\mathsf{J}^{\flat}_{u,v,p.C}(u.d;b_{1},b_{2},q):C[b_{1}/u,b_{2}/v,q/p]}\quad\textsc{right=(\ref{thm:crisp-J})}\mathsf{J}^{\flat}_{u,v,p.C}(u.d;u,u,\mathsf{refl}_{u})\equiv d

Figure 9.
Some crisp induction principles

A more or less equivalent way to state
5.4
is that
\flat
preserves coproducts.
This is sensible from a category-theoretic point of view, since we will soon see that
\flat
is left adjoint to
\sharp
.

Corollary 5.5
.

\flat A+\flat B\simeq\flat(A+B)
for any crisp types
A
and
B
.

Proof.

To map from left to right, we do ordinary case analysis and then use the functoriality of
\flat
.
From right to left, we do
\flat
-induction, then crisp case analysis, then apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
in both cases.
We leave the details to the reader, along with the proof that these are inverses.
∎

We can apply the same method to other positive types such as
\emptyset
and
\mathbb{N}
, concluding in particular that
\flat\emptyset\simeq\emptyset
and
\flat\mathbb{N}\simeq\mathbb{N}
.
It also applies to identity types:

Theorem 5.6

(Crisp Id-induction)
.

Suppose
C::\mathsf{Type}
depends on
u,v::B
and
p::u=v
, and that we have
d::C[u/v,\mathsf{refl}_{u}/p]
depending on
u::B
.
Then for any
u,v::B
and
p::u=v
we have
\mathsf{J}^{\flat}_{u,v,p.C}(u.d;u,v,p):C
, such that
\mathsf{J}^{\flat}_{u,v,p.C}(u.d;u,u,\mathsf{refl}_{u})\equiv d
.

Proof.

As before, if we have cohesive variables
x,y:B
and
q:x=y
we can write
\sharp C[x/u,y/v,q/p]
. Now we apply ordinary Id-induction: to inhabit
\sharp C[x/u,y/v,q/p]
for all
x,y:A
and
q:x=y
, it suffices to inhabit
\sharp C[x/u,x/v,\mathsf{refl}_{x}/p]
for all
x:A
.
But such an inhabitant is supplied by
d[x/u]^{\sharp}
(note that
u
is crisp in
d
, but inside
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}
we can assume
x
to be crisp).

We have shown that for all
x,y:A
and
q:x=y
, we have

\mathsf{J}_{x,y,q.\sharp C[x/u,y/v,q/p]}(x.d[x/u]^{\sharp};x,y,q)\;:\;\sharp C[x/u,y/v,q/p].

Now if we in fact have
crisp
assumptions
u,v::A
and
p::u=v
, then this becomes a crisp conclusion

\mathsf{J}_{x,y,q.\sharp C[x/u,y/v,q/p]}(x.d[x/u]^{\sharp};u,v,p)\;::\;\sharp C

(since
C
and
d
are also crisp).
Thus, we can apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\sharp}
to obtain an element of
C
.
Finally, if we substitute
(u,u,\mathsf{refl}_{u})
for
(u,v,p)
, we have

\mathsf{J}_{x,y,q.\sharp C(x,y,q)}(x.d[x/u]^{\sharp};u,u,\mathsf{refl}_{u})_{\sharp}\equiv d^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}\equiv d.\qed

In particular, we can transport in a crisp type along a crisp identification: if
C::\mathsf{Type}
depends on
z::A
and we have
a,b::A
and
p::a=b
and
c::C[a/z]
, then we have
p_{**}(c)::C[b/z]
.
We repeat that the important novelty here is that the definition of
C
might use the crispness of
z
.
Note that since crisp Id-induction is defined using an ordinary Id-induction followed by
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\sharp}
, the uniqueness rule for
\sharp
implies
p_{**}(c)^{\sharp}=p_{*}(c^{\sharp})
.

With “crisp transport”, we can state and prove crisp induction principles for some higher inductive types as well, and particularly for homotopy colimits.

Theorem 5.7

(Crisp
\mathsf{coeq}
-induction)
.

Let
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
and
f,g::A\to B
with homotopy coequalizer
q::B\to\mathsf{coeq}(f,g)
and
p::\mathchoice{\prod_{u:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:A)}}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:A)}}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:A)}}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}{\prod_{(u:A)}}}q(f(u))=q(g(u))
.
Let
C::\mathsf{Type}
depend on
z::\mathsf{coeq}(f,g)
, let
N::C[q(v)/z]
depend on
v::B
, and let
P::p(u)_{**}(N[q(f(u))/z])=N[q(g(u))/z]
depend on
u::A
.
Then for any
M::\mathsf{coeq}(f,g)
we have an element of
C[M/z]
, which computes to
N
on
q
and to
P
on
f,g
.

Proof.

For a cohesive variable
z:\mathsf{coeq}(f,g)
we can write
\sharp C:\mathsf{Type}
, and apply ordinary
\mathsf{coeq}
-induction.
We also have
N^{\sharp}:\sharp C[q(v)/z]
depending on a cohesive
v:B
.
As remarked above, we have
p(u)_{*}(N[q(f(u))/z]^{\sharp})=p(u)_{**}(N[q(f(u))/z])^{\sharp}
.
Now both
p(u)_{**}(N[q(f(u))/z])^{\sharp}
and
N[q(g(u))/z]^{\sharp}
belong to
\sharp C[q(g(u))/z]
, so by the proof of
3.7
we have

\displaystyle\Big(p(u)_{**}(N[q(f(u))/z])^{\sharp}

\displaystyle=N[q(g(u))/z]^{\sharp}\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\quad\sharp\Big(p(u)_{**}(N[q(f(u))/z])^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}

\displaystyle=N[q(g(u))/z]^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}\Big)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\quad\sharp\Big(p(u)_{**}(N[q(f(u))/z])

\displaystyle=N[q(g(u))/z]\Big)

which is the type of
P^{\sharp}
.
Thus we have the inputs to ordinary
\mathsf{coeq}
-induction, after which we can apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\sharp}
as usual.
∎

Similarly, we have crisp induction for
S^{1}
, for pushouts, and so on.
The case of truncations requires a little more thought.

Theorem 5.8

(Crisp truncation-induction)
.

Let
n::\mathbb{N}
and
A::\mathsf{Type}
, let
C
be a crisp
n
-type depending on
z::\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|_{n}\mathclose{}
, and let
N::C[\mathopen{}\left|x\right|_{n}\mathclose{}/z]
depend on
x::A
.
For any
M::\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|_{n}\mathclose{}
, we have an element of
C[M/z]
, which computes to
N
on
|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}|_{n}
.

Proof.

For a cohesive variable
z:\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|_{n}\mathclose{}
, we have
\sharp C:\mathsf{Type}
, to which we apply ordinary truncation-induction.
We have
N^{\sharp}:\sharp C[\mathopen{}\left|x\right|_{n}\mathclose{}/z]
for a cohesive
x:A
; so it remains to show that
\sharp C
is an
n
-type.
But in
section

3
we showed that
\sharp
preserves
n
-types, and it is straightforward to generalize this (using crisp
\mathbb{N}
-induction) to a derivable rule in which
n
is crisp and the input
n
-type
C
depends on crisp variables (that become cohesive in
\sharp C
).
Thus ordinary truncation-induction applies, after which we use
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\sharp}
as usual.
∎

In the sequent calculus of
Licata & Shulman 2016
, these crisp induction principles are taken as given, rather than derived from
\sharp
.
This produces a better-behaved calculus in a proof-theoretic sense.
However, not
all
(higher) inductive types can consistently admit a crisp induction principle; we will see a counterexample in
section

9
.
For this reason, I prefer to derive all such principles from
\sharp
.

6.
Discreteness and
\flat
revisited

6.1.
Identifications in
\flat

Just as we showed in
3.7
that
\sharp
is left exact using an encode-decode argument, we can do the same for
\flat
.

Theorem 6.1
.

For any
x,y::A
we have
(x^{\flat}=y^{\flat})\simeq\flat(x=y)
.

Proof.

We define
\mathsf{code}:\flat A\to\flat A\to\mathsf{Type}
by

\mathsf{code}(u,v)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;y^{\flat}\coloneqq v\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(x=y))).

Then for any
x,y::A
,
\mathsf{code}(x^{\flat},y^{\flat})\equiv\flat(x=y)
.
Thus, it will suffice to prove that for any
u,v:\flat A
we have
\mathsf{code}(u,v)\simeq(u=v)
.

First note that for any
u:\flat A
, we have

\mathsf{r}(u)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{refl}_{x})^{\flat}):\mathsf{code}(u,u).

As usual, we then define
\mathsf{encode}:\mathchoice{\prod_{u,v:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}(u=v)\to\mathsf{code}(u,v)
by
\mathsf{encode}(p)\vcentcolon\equiv p_{*}\mathsf{r}(u)
.

Second, to construct
\mathsf{decode}:\mathchoice{\prod_{u,v:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u,v:\flat A)}}}\mathsf{code}(u,v)\to(u=v)
, if we destruct
u
and
v
as
x^{\flat}
and
y^{\flat}
respectively with
x,y::A
, we obtain
c:\flat(x=y)
with a goal of
(x^{\flat}=y^{\flat})
.
Then we can further destruct
c
as a crisp
p::x=y
.
Now our assumptions of
x
,
y
, and
p
suffice to apply crisp Id-induction,
5.6
; this reduces our goal to
(x^{\flat}=x^{\flat})
for a given
x::A
.
But now of course we can use
\mathsf{refl}_{x^{\flat}}
.
In symbols, we have

\mathsf{decode}_{u,v}(c)\vcentcolon\equiv\\
(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;y^{\flat}\coloneqq v\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;p^{\flat}\coloneqq c\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{J}^{\flat}_{x,y,p.(x^{\flat}=y^{\flat})}(x.\mathsf{refl}_{x^{\flat}};x,y,p)))).

Now as remarked in
3.7
, it suffices to show that for any
u,v:\flat A
and
c:\mathsf{code}(u,v)
we have
\mathsf{encode}(\mathsf{decode}(c))=c
.
Of course, we destruct
u,v,c
as
x,y::A
and
p::x=y
, and then apply crisp Id-induction; the goal then becomes
(\mathsf{refl}_{x^{\flat}})_{*}(\mathsf{refl}_{x}{}^{\flat})=\mathsf{refl}_{x}{}^{\flat}
, which is true.
∎

Corollary 6.2
.

For any
u,v::\flat A
, we have
(u=v)\simeq\flat(u_{\flat}=v_{\flat})
.

Proof.

We showed above that any crisp element
u::\flat A
is equal to
u_{\flat}{}^{\flat}
, so this follows from
6.1
.
∎

Note that
u
and
v
in the statement of
section

6.1
must be crisp for the right-hand side to make sense.
However, inspecting the proof of
6.1
we extract a more general statement that applies even to cohesive variables
x,y:\flat A
, namely

(x=y)\simeq(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;y^{\flat}\coloneqq v\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(x=y))).

6.1
has many useful consequences.
For instance, it implies the functoriality of
\flat
on homotopies.
That is, suppose that
f,g::A\to B
and
H::f\sim g
is a crisp homotopy, i.e.
H::\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}f(x)=g(x)
; we would like to conclude
\flat f\sim\flat g
.
Introducing
x:\flat A
and using
\flat
-induction, this reduces to saying that for any
u::A
we have
f(u)^{\flat}=g(u)^{\flat}
.
This
looks
like it should be immediate from
H(u)::f(u)=g(u)
, but since
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
is not a
function
, we cannot write “
\mathsf{ap}_{(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}}(H(u))
”.
Instead we use the following.

Corollary 6.3
.

For any
u,v::B
and
p::u=v
, we have
p^{=\flat}:u^{\flat}=v^{\flat}
such that
(\mathsf{refl}_{u})^{=\flat}=\mathsf{refl}_{u^{\flat}}
and
\mathsf{ap}_{(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}}(p^{=\flat})=p
.

Proof.

We apply the
\mathsf{decode}
from
6.1
to
p^{\flat}:\flat(u=v)
.
The first equality
(\mathsf{refl}_{u})^{=\flat}=\mathsf{refl}_{u^{\flat}}
follows from the definition of
\mathsf{decode}
.
For the second, we use crisp Id-induction on
p
, and observe that

\mathsf{ap}_{(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}}((\mathsf{refl}_{u})^{=\flat})=\mathsf{ap}_{(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}}(\mathsf{refl}_{u^{\flat}})=\mathsf{refl}_{u}

using the first equality.
∎

Now we can define the action of
\flat
on a homotopy
H
by

\flat H(x)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;H(u)^{=\flat}).

Functoriality on homotopies also implies that the functor
\flat
preserves (crisp) equivalences: if
e::A\simeq B
, then we have
\flat e:\flat A\simeq\flat B
.

We can also regard
6.1
as the corollary of
5.6
saying analogously to
section

5

that “
\flat
preserves identity types”.
It implies analogous corollaries of
5.7
and
5.8
.

Corollary 6.4
.

For any
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
and
f,g::A\to B
, we have
\flat(\mathsf{coeq}(f,g))\simeq\mathsf{coeq}(\flat f,\flat g)
.

Proof.

From left to right, we apply
\flat
-induction, crisp
\mathsf{coeq}
-induction, and then
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
and the structure maps of
\mathsf{coeq}(\flat f,\flat g)
.
From right to left, we apply ordinary
\mathsf{coeq}
-induction first, and then
\flat q:\flat B\to\flat(\mathsf{coeq}(f,g))
; finally we need to show that for
x:\flat A
we have
\flat q(\flat f(x))=\flat q(\flat g(x))
; but this follows from the above functoriality of
\flat
on homotopies.
That the composites are identities is proven similarly.
∎

Corollary 6.5
.

\flat S^{1}\simeq S^{1}
.∎

Theorem 6.6
.

For any
n::\mathbb{N}
and
A::\mathsf{Type}
, if
A
is crisply an
n
-type, so is
\flat A
.

Proof.

We do crisp induction (as in
section

5
) over
n::\mathbb{N}
, beginning with
n=-2
.
If
A
is crisply contractible, we have some
c::A
, hence also
c^{\flat}:\flat A
.
Now any
x:\flat A
can be destructed as
u^{\flat}
for
u::A
, and by
6.1

(u^{\flat}=a^{\flat})\simeq\flat(u=a)
.
But
A
is crisply contractible, so we have
p::u=a
, hence
p^{\flat}:\flat(u=a)
.

For the induction step, suppose
A
is crisply an
(n+1)
-type.
Then
x,y:\flat A
can be destructed as
u^{\flat}
and
v^{\flat}
for
u,v::A
, and we have
(u^{\flat}=v^{\flat})\simeq\flat(u=v)
.
But
u=v
is crisply an
n
-type; hence by the inductive hypothesis so is
\flat(u=v)
.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

For
n::\mathbb{N}
and
A::\mathsf{Type}
, we have
\flat\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|_{n}\mathclose{}\simeq\mathopen{}\left\|\flat A\right\|_{n}\mathclose{}
.

Proof.

From left to right, we apply
\flat
-induction and crisp truncation-induction, followed by
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\flat}
.
From right to left, we apply ordinary truncation-induction (using
6.6
) and then functoriality of
\flat
on
|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}|_{n}
.
The composites on either side are proven to be identities similarly.
∎

Thirdly,
6.1
implies
\flat
is left exact, using an analogue of
section

3
:

Lemma 6.8
.

For any
A::\mathsf{Type}
and
B::A\to\mathsf{Type}
, we have

\flat\left(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)\right)\simeq\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat B(u)\big).

Proof.

From left to right, we first apply
\flat
-induction, then destruct to get a crisp
u::A
and
v::B(u)
.
Then we can form
u^{\flat}
and
v^{\flat}:\flat B(u)
, and
(u^{\flat},v^{\flat})
belongs to the right-hand side by the computation rule for
\flat
.
From right to left, we first destruct into
x:\flat A
and
y:(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat B(u))
.
Then we apply
\flat
-induction to
x
to get
u::A
, reducing the type of
y
to
\flat B(u)
.
Next we apply
\flat
-induction to
y
to get
v::B(u)
, so we can form
