(u,v)^{\flat}:\flat\left(\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)\right)
.
We leave it to the reader to check that these are inverses.
∎

Theorem 6.9
.

For any
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
we have
\flat(A\times B)\simeq\flat A\times\flat B
.

Proof.

If
B
is independent of
u:A
, then
(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat B)=\flat B
(this can be regarded as an instance of the uniqueness principle for
\flat
).
∎

Theorem 6.10
.

For any
A,B,C::\mathsf{Type}
with
f::A\to C
and
g::B\to C
, we have
\flat(A\times_{C}B)\simeq\flat A\times_{\flat C}\flat B
.

Proof.

We compute

\displaystyle\flat(A\times_{C}B)

\displaystyle\equiv\flat\Big(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}(f(x)=g(y))\Big)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(f(u)=g(v))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}\flat(f(x_{\flat})=g(y_{\flat}))

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}\flat(\flat f(x)_{\flat}=\flat g(y)_{\flat})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}{\sum_{(y:\flat B)}}(\flat f(x)=\flat g(y))

\displaystyle\equiv\flat A\times_{\flat C}\flat B.

The first step is the definition of pullbacks and the second is
section

6.1
.
The third step combines several commuting conversions and the fourth is naturality of
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}
.
The fifth step is
section

6.1
and the last is the definition of pullbacks.
∎

For completeness, we should also record:

Theorem 6.11
.

\flat\mathbf{1}\simeq\mathbf{1}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\flat\mathbf{1}
is contractible.
Since we have
\mathsf{tt}^{\flat}:\flat\mathbf{1}
, it suffices to show that
\flat\mathbf{1}
is a proposition.
Now if
u,v:\flat\mathbf{1}
, we can destruct them as
x^{\flat}
and
y^{\flat}
for
x::\mathbf{1}
and
y::\mathbf{1}
.
Since
\mathbf{1}
is (crisply) contractible we have
p::x=y
, whence
p^{\flat}:\flat(x=y)
.
Thus, by
6.1
, we have
x^{\flat}=y^{\flat}
.
∎

6.2.
Discrete types

We now move on to study discrete types, the duals of the codiscrete types from
section

3
.
The definition should come as no surprise.

Definition 6.12
.

A crisp type
A::\mathsf{Type}
is
discrete
if
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A\to A
is an equivalence.

Remark 6.13
.

To say that a type is
discrete
is very different from saying that it is
crisp
.
A discrete type is one whose
points
are “completely unconnected” topologically, whereas
a crisp type is one for which we can ignore the topology relating it to
other types
.
Discreteness is an “intrinsic” aspect of a type, whereas crispness is a more “syntactic” one indicating what we are doing with that type at the moment.
Roughly speaking, a type is discrete if we are free to assume that its
elements
are crisp.

At this point in our development, a type must be crisp before we can even ask whether it is discrete.
However, in
section

8
we will introduce a more general notion of discreteness that removes this restriction.

Note that a (crisp and) discrete type
A::\mathsf{Type}
might also be
crisply discrete
, i.e. we might have a
crisp
witness
d::\mathsf{isdiscrete}(A)
.
This distinction is admittedly somewhat confusing.
In practice, pretty much every discrete type is crisply discrete, and whenever we assume a type to be discrete it must be crisply discrete.
Moreover, in
section

8
we will introduce a further axiom ensuring that
every
discrete type is crisply discrete.

Our first goal is to show that the discrete types form a
coreflective
subuniverse in an appropriate sense, with
\flat
as the coreflector.
The core of that result is that maps
\flat B\to A
are equivalent to maps
\flat B\to\flat A
; this should appear sensible since any function out of a discrete space is continuous.
We do, however, have to apply a further
\flat
on both sides, since the type
\flat B\to A
may inherit a nontrivial topology from
A
.

Theorem 6.14
.

For any
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
, postcomposition with
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A\to A
induces an equivalence

(6.1)

\flat(\flat B\to\flat A)\simeq\flat(\flat B\to A).

Proof.

To be precise, the left-to-right map in (
6.1
) is defined as follows: given
h:\flat(\flat B\to\flat A)
, we first destruct it into
k::\flat B\to\flat A
.
Now since we have only crisp variables, it suffices to construct a function
\flat B\to A
, for which we have
{\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat B).\,k(x)_{\flat}
.
In symbols, from
h:\flat(\flat B\to\flat A)
we construct

\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat B).\,k(x)_{\flat})^{\flat}\quad:\flat(\flat B\to A).

To define the right-to-left map in (
6.1
), suppose given an element of
\flat(\flat B\to A)
, which we immediately destruct into
f::\flat B\to A
.
To construct an element of
\flat(\flat B\to\flat A)
, since we have only crisp variables it suffices to construct a function
\flat B\to\flat A
.
Thus, let
x:\flat B
, which we immediately destruct into
u::B
.
But now again it suffices to construct an element of
A
itself, for which we can take
f(u^{\flat})
.
In symbols, from
g:\flat(\flat B\to A)
we construct

\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat B).\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})^{\flat}\quad:\flat(\flat B\to\flat A).

It is tempting to think that the uniqueness principle for
\flat
(
section

4.2
) should simplify “
\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat}
” to
f(x)^{\flat}
.
However, this rule does not apply because we cannot write
f(x)^{\flat}
, since
x
is not a crisp variable.

In
fig.

10
we verify that both round-trip composites are the identity.
We use the fact that since
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}
is defined with
\mathsf{let}
, the commuting conversions allow us to push it inside another
\mathsf{let}
, along with function extensionality to allow this (and the uniqueness principle) to happen inside a
\lambda
-abstraction.
∎

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})^{\flat})\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,k(x)_{\flat})^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,k(x)_{\flat})^{\flat})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})_{\flat})^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat}{}_{\flat})^{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat}))^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,f(x))^{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;f^{\flat}

\displaystyle=g.

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,k(x)_{\flat})^{\flat})\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq({\lambda}x.\,k(x)_{\flat})^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})^{\flat})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;k(u^{\flat})_{\flat}{}^{\flat})^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;k(u^{\flat}))^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}x.\,k(x))^{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;k^{\flat}

\displaystyle=h.

Figure 10.
The round-trip composites in
6.14

Corollary 6.15
.

If
B::\mathsf{Type}
is crisply discrete, then for any
A::\mathsf{Type}
, postcomposition with
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A\to A
induces an equivalence

(6.2)

\flat(B\to\flat A)\simeq\flat(B\to A).

Proof.

As remarked after
section

6.1
, the functor
\flat
preserves equivalences, and precomposition with the equivalence
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat B\simeq B
is an equivalence on function types.
Thus, this follows immediately from
6.14
.
∎

More generally, we have a dependent universal property:

Theorem 6.16
.

For
B::\mathsf{Type}
and
A::\flat B\to\mathsf{Type}
, fiberwise postcomposition with
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A(x)\to A(x)
induces an equivalence

(6.3)

\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\flat B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(A(u^{\flat})))\right)\simeq\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\flat B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}A(x)\right)

Therefore, if
B::\mathsf{Type}
is crisply discrete and
A::B\to\mathsf{Type}
, fiberwise postcomposition with
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A(x)\to A(x)
induces an equivalence

(6.4)

\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq s(x)\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(A(u)))\right)\simeq\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:B)}}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}{\prod_{(x:B)}}}A(x)\right)

where
s:B\to\flat B
is the inverse of
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat B\to B
.

Proof.

The left-to-right map in (
6.3
) is defined like that in (
6.1
), taking
h
to

\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq h\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat B).\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;k(u^{\flat})_{\flat})^{\flat}\quad:\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\flat B}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat B)}}}A(x)\right).

Its inverse looks exactly the same as the inverse of (
6.1
), though now all the functions are dependent:

\mathsf{let}\;f^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat B).\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;f(u^{\flat})^{\flat})^{\flat}\quad:\flat(\flat B\to\flat A).

The calculations in
fig.

10
generalize immediately, and (
6.4
) is again obtained by transporting along the equivalence
\flat B\simeq B
.
∎

Lemma 6.17
.

For
A::\mathsf{Type}
to be crisply discrete, it suffices that
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}:\flat A\to A
have a crisp section.

Proof.

First we should clarify what is meant by a “crisp section”.
The type of sections of
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}
is
\mathchoice{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}{\sum_{(s:A\to\flat A)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(a:A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}s(a)_{\flat}=a
.
Having a crisp element of this type means having a crisp
s::A\to\flat A
and also a crisp homotopy
H::\mathchoice{\prod_{a:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(a:A)}}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}{\prod_{(a:A)}}}s(a)_{\flat}=a
.

It remains to show that
s(x_{\flat})=x
for all
x:\flat A
.
By
\flat
-induction, it suffices to show that for any
u::A
we have
s(u^{\flat}{}_{\flat})=u^{\flat}
.
But
u^{\flat}{}_{\flat}\equiv u
, so what we must show is
s(u)=u^{\flat}
.
Now
s(u):\flat A
is crisp, so we also have
s(u)=s(u)_{\flat}{}^{\flat}
; thus it suffices to show
s(u)_{\flat}{}^{\flat}=u^{\flat}
.
But now
section

6.1
tells us that this follows from
H(u)::s(u)_{\flat}=u
.
∎

Theorem 6.18
.

For any
A::\mathsf{Type}
, the type
\flat A
is crisply discrete.

Proof.

We define
s:\flat A\to\flat\flat A
by
s(x)\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat}{}^{\flat})
.
Applying
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}
to
s(x)
, we get

\quad(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat}{}^{\flat})_{\flat}=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat}{}^{\flat}{}_{\flat})\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat})=x

using a commuting conversion and the uniqueness rule.
∎

Thus
\flat
coreflects into the discrete types, dually to how
\sharp
reflects into the codiscrete types.
However, there are several ways in which the duality breaks down.
Firstly there is the outer
\flat
on the equivalence in
section

6.2
.

Secondly, we have seen that
\sharp
and
\flat
are
both
left exact, whereas a strict duality would interchange left exactness with right exactness.
Note that exactness of reflectors and coreflectors can be a bit subtle and confusing.
For instance, a reflector is a left adjoint, so it always preserves colimits, i.e. takes colimits in the ambient category to colimits
in the reflective subcategory
— but these latter colimits will only coincide with those in the ambient category if the subcategory is closed under them.
This is not generally the case for
\sharp
: the coproduct of codiscrete spaces is not generally codiscrete.

Dually, a coreflector is a right adjoint, so it always takes limits in the ambient category to limits in the coreflective subcategory.
The content of
6.9
,
6.10
and
6.11
is thus that the discrete types
are
closed under finite limits.
We record these facts:

Theorem 6.19
.

If
A,B,C::\mathsf{Type}
are crisply discrete and
f::A\to C
and
g::B\to C
and
x,y::A
, then the following types are crisply discrete:

A\times BA\times_{C}B\mathbf{1}(x=y)

Proof.

For
A\times B
, we have
A\times B\simeq\flat A\times\flat B\simeq\flat(A\times B)
.
The case of
A\times_{c}B
is similar, and
\mathbf{1}
is exactly
6.11
.
For
x=y
, since equivalences lift to identity types, it suffices to show that if
x,y::\flat A
then
(x=y)
is discrete;
but this follows from
6.18
and
6.1
.
∎

More generally, we have:

Theorem 6.20
.

Suppose
A::\mathsf{Type}
is crisply discrete, and that
B::A\to\mathsf{Type}
is such that for every
x:\flat A
, we have
(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{isdiscrete}(B(u)))
.
Then
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)
is crisply discrete.

The hypothesis is morally “
B(u)
is discrete for every
u::A
”, but stating it that way would require this theorem to be only a “derivable rule” (see
section

4.2
), since we cannot say “for every
u::A
” in an ordinary hypothesis.

Proof.

Using
section

6.1
, we have

\displaystyle\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}B(x)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat B(u)\big)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}\big(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;B(u)\big)\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}{\sum_{(x:\flat A)}}B(x_{\flat})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(u:A)}}}{\sum_{(u:A)}}{\sum_{(u:A)}}{\sum_{(u:A)}}B(u).\qed

On the other hand, the dual of left-exactness of
\sharp
would be right-exactness of
\flat
, which is true.
In fact, as remarked in
section

5
, this is essentially the content of the crisp induction principles.
We record these facts:

Theorem 6.21
.

If
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
are crisply discrete and
f,g::A\to B
and
n::\mathbb{N}
, then the following types are crisply discrete:

A+B\emptyset\mathbb{N}S^{1}\mathsf{coeq}(f,g)\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|_{n}\mathclose{}

Combining the last two, set-coequalizers also preserve crisp discreteness.∎

6.3.
Adjointness of
\flat
and
\sharp

To complete our study of
\flat
, we consider how it interacts with
\sharp
.
Our first observation is that
\flat
and
\sharp
“eat each other”.

Theorem 6.22
.

For any
A::\mathsf{Type}
, there are natural equivalences

\sharp\flat A\simeq\sharp A\qquad\text{and}\qquad\flat A\simeq\flat\sharp A.

This is sensible according to our topological intuition:
\flat
and
\sharp
are supposed to re-equip a space with the discrete or codiscrete topology, respectively, so it shouldn’t matter whether we have already modified its topology in the other way.

Proof.

The first equivalence is defined by

{\lambda}(y\,{:}\,\sharp\flat A).\,y_{\sharp}{}_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\sharp A).\,x_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}{}^{\sharp}.

The round-trip composite starting with
x:\sharp A
yields

x_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}{}^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}{}_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}\equiv x_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}{}_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}\equiv x_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}\equiv x

while that starting with
y:\sharp\flat A
yields

y_{\sharp}{}_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}{}^{\sharp}\equiv y_{\sharp}{}_{\flat}{}^{\flat}{}^{\sharp}=y_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}\equiv y.

The second equivalence is defined by

{\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat A).\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\sharp}{}^{\flat}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\lambda}(y\,{:}\,\flat\sharp A).\,\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;v_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}.

The round-trip composite starting with
x:\flat A
yields

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\sharp}{}^{\flat})\;\mathsf{in}\;v_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq u^{\sharp}{}^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;v_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\flat}

\displaystyle=u.

And the round-trip composite starting with
y:\flat\sharp A
yields

\displaystyle\quad\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq(\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;v_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\sharp}{}^{\flat}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq v_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;u^{\sharp}{}^{\flat})

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;v_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}{}^{\flat}

\displaystyle\equiv\mathsf{let}\;v^{\flat}\coloneqq y\;\mathsf{in}\;v^{\flat}

\displaystyle=v.\qed

Corollary 6.23
.

For any crisply discrete
A::\mathsf{Type}
, the map
A\to\flat\sharp A
is an equivalence.
Dually, for any crisply codiscrete
A::\mathsf{Type}
, the map
\sharp\flat A\to A
is an equivalence.∎

We can express this more internally using the type
\mathsf{Codisc}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\mathsf{Type})}}\mathsf{iscodiscrete}(A)
from
3.11
and an analogous universe of discrete types, defined as:

\mathsf{Disc}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{\sum_{A:\flat\mathsf{Type}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}(\mathsf{let}\;B^{\flat}\coloneqq A\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{isdiscrete}(B)).

Corollary
{UA}
6.24
.

\mathsf{Disc}\simeq\flat\mathsf{Codisc}
; hence also
\sharp\mathsf{Disc}\simeq\mathsf{Codisc}
.

Proof.

By
section

6.1
, we have

\flat\mathsf{Codisc}\simeq\mathchoice{\sum_{A:\flat\mathsf{Type}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\sum_{(A:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}(\mathsf{let}\;B^{\flat}\coloneqq A\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{iscodiscrete}(B)).

The equivalence
\mathsf{Disc}\simeq\flat\mathsf{Codisc}
now comes from the endomaps of
\flat\mathsf{Type}
induced by
\flat
and
\sharp
.
These are mutually inverse by
sections

6.3
and
6.1
and univalence.
∎

Since
\sharp
and
\flat
preserve
n
-types, this equivalence relativizes to the universes of
n
-types.
In particular, we have
\mathsf{DiscProp}\simeq\flat\mathsf{CodiscProp}
, which uses only propositional univalence.

Furthermore, this equivalence respects all type formers, suitably reflected or coreflected.
For instance, since discrete and codiscrete types are closed under cartesian products, we have product operations
\mathsf{Disc}\to\mathsf{Disc}\to\mathsf{Disc}
and
\mathsf{Codisc}\to\mathsf{Codisc}\to\mathsf{Codisc}
, and these commute with the equivalence of
section

6.3
.
Discrete objects need not be closed under function types, but we have an operation
\mathsf{Disc}\to\mathsf{Disc}\to\mathsf{Disc}
defined by

{\lambda}A.\,{\lambda}B.\,\mathsf{let}\;C^{\flat}\coloneqq A\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{let}\;D^{\flat}\coloneqq B\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(C\to D)

which agrees with
{\lambda}A.\,{\lambda}B.\,(A\to B):\mathsf{Codisc}\to\mathsf{Codisc}\to\mathsf{Codisc}
under the equivalence of
section

6.3
; and so on.

Topological intuition also suggests that since all functions between discrete or codiscrete spaces are continuous, both
\flat A\to\flat B
and
\sharp A\to\sharp B
ought to be the set of all (discontinuous) functions from
A
to
B
.
These two spaces may themselves have different topologies, of course (and in fact they do), but if we make them both discrete or codiscrete we should be able to identify them.

Theorem 6.25
.

For any
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
, there is an equivalence

(6.5)

\flat(\flat A\to\flat B)\simeq\flat(\sharp A\to\sharp B).

Proof.

On one hand, we have the functorial action of
\sharp
:

\sharp\;:\;(\flat A\to\flat B)\to(\sharp\flat A\to\sharp\flat B).

We can compose the result with the equivalences
\sharp\flat A\simeq\sharp A
and
\sharp\flat B\simeq\sharp B
on either side, and apply then the functor
\flat
, to obtain a map from left to right in (
6.5
).
On the other hand, as remarked above, the functorial action of
\flat
can be expressed (in this case) as

\flat\;:\;\flat(\sharp A\to\sharp B)\to\flat(\flat\sharp A\to\flat\sharp B)

and we can again compose on either side with the equivalences
\flat\sharp A\simeq\flat A
and
\flat\sharp B\simeq\flat B
.

More explicitly, given
f:\flat(\flat A\to\flat B)
we can write

\mathsf{let}\;h^{\flat}\coloneqq f\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\sharp A).\,h(x_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})_{\flat}{}^{\sharp})^{\flat}\;:\flat(\sharp A\to\sharp B)

and given
g:\flat(\sharp A\to\sharp B)
we can write

\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,\flat A).\,\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;k(u^{\sharp})_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})^{\flat}\;:\flat(\flat A\to\flat B).

Omitting the commuting conversions and reductions that eliminate the variables
h
and
k
, as in the proof of
6.14
, the computations that show these to be inverses are

\displaystyle(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;k(u^{\sharp})_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}\equiv k(x_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp})_{\sharp}{}^{\flat}{}_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}\equiv k(x_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp})_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}\equiv k(x)_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}\equiv k(x)

\displaystyle(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;h(u^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})_{\flat}{}^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp}{}^{\flat})\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;h(u^{\flat})_{\flat}{}^{\flat})=(\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;h(u^{\flat}))=h(x).\qed

Corollary 6.26
.

For any
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
, there is a natural equivalence

\flat(\flat A\to B)\simeq\flat(A\to\sharp B).

In other words,
\flat
is “crisply left adjoint” to
\sharp
.

Proof.

We have the following chain of equivalences:

\flat(\flat A\to B)\simeq\flat(\flat A\to\flat B)\simeq\flat(\sharp A\to\sharp B)\simeq\flat(A\to\sharp B).\qed

In fact, more generally we can say:

Theorem 6.27
.

For any
A::\mathsf{Type}
and
B::A\to\mathsf{Type}
, there is a natural equivalence

\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}B(u_{\flat})\right)\simeq\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}\sharp B(x)\right).

Proof.

Given
f:\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{u:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}B(u_{\flat})\right)
, we define
g:\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp B(x)\right)
by

\mathsf{let}\;h^{\flat}\coloneqq f\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(x\,{:}\,A).\,h(x^{\flat})^{\sharp})^{\flat}

and given
g
, we define
f
by

\mathsf{let}\;k^{\flat}\coloneqq g\;\mathsf{in}\;({\lambda}(u\,{:}\,\flat A).\,\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;k(x)_{\sharp})^{\flat}.

Omitting the outer commuting conversions and reductions again, the computations that show these to be inverses are

(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;h(x^{\flat})^{\sharp}{}_{\sharp})\equiv(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;h(x^{\flat}))\equiv h(u)

and

(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq x^{\flat}\;\mathsf{in}\;k(x)_{\sharp})^{\sharp}\equiv k(x)_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}\equiv k(x).\qed

Hence we have both a reflective subcategory (the codiscrete types) and a coreflective subcategory (the discrete types), which by
section

6.3
are abstractly equivalent via the reflection and coreflection.
Moreover,
6.22
tells us that modulo this equivalence, the reflector
\sharp
agrees with the coreflector
\flat
, which is sensible if we consider that both take the same underlying set (or
\infty
-groupoid) and equip it with a new topology.

Remark 6.28
.

We now have another formulation of the law of excluded middle.
When assumed as an axiom,
section

3.1
is a
crisp
element of
\mathchoice{\prod_{P:\mathsf{Prop}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}\sharp(P\vee\neg P)
, and hence yields an element of
\flat\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{P:\mathsf{Prop}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\mathsf{Prop})}}}\sharp(P\vee\neg P)\right)
.
Thus, by
6.27
we can obtain a (crisp) element of

(6.6)

\mathchoice{\prod_{P:\flat\mathsf{Prop}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}{\prod_{(P:\flat\mathsf{Prop})}}}(P_{\flat}\vee\neg P_{\flat}),

a statement which we might call the
flat law of excluded middle
.

Remark 6.29
.

The overall conclusion of
sections

4
to
6
can be summarized as “
\flat
is a coreflector, and the images of
\flat
and
\sharp
are equivalent by an equivalence that identifies
\flat
with
\sharp
.”
But in contrast to the analogous statement about
\sharp
in
section

3
, this cannot be stated purely cohesively, because of the no-go
4.1
.
In
Schreiber & Shulman 2012
, using ordinary type theory without crispness, we stated this axiomatically with
\flat
as an operation on
\sharp\mathsf{Type}
; here we instead derive it from the rules governing
\flat
and
\sharp
in relation to crisp hypotheses.

6.4.
The axiom of choice revisited

Let us now return to the sharp axiom of choice,
Axiom AC
.
Restated, this says that for any set
A
and type family
P:A\to\mathsf{Type}
, we have

\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)\to\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}.

Using discreteness, we can derive from this a statement less encumbered by
\sharp
s.

Theorem
{AC}
6.30

(The discrete axiom of choice)
.

Suppose
A::\mathsf{Type}
is a crisply discrete set, that
P::A\to\mathsf{Type}
is also crisp, and that
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
crisply.
Then
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Proof.

The hypothesis
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\mathopen{}\left\|P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
is stronger than that of the sharp axiom of choice, so we can apply the latter, obtaining
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Since all the parameters are crisp, we can apply
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\sharp}
to get
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}\sharp P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
; whereas what we want is
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(x)\right\|\mathclose{}
.
By functoriality, we can remove the
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\right\|\mathclose{}
from both.
Now by
6.27
, we get
\mathchoice{\prod_{u:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(u:\flat A)}}}P(u_{\flat})
; but
A
is discrete, so this gives
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(x)
as desired.
∎

Corollary
{AC}
6.31

(The crisp countable axiom of choice)
.

Suppose
P::\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{Type}
is crisp and that
\mathchoice{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}\mathopen{}\left\|P(n)\right\|\mathclose{}
crisply.
Then
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}P(n)\right\|\mathclose{}
.∎

We will refer to this statement as
6.4
.
As mentioned previously, later on we will assume an axiom ensuring that all propositions are discrete; thus the crispness of
\mathchoice{\prod_{n:\mathbb{N}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\prod_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}\mathopen{}\left\|P(n)\right\|\mathclose{}
will become automatic.
However, the crispness of
P
is a real restriction that makes this significantly weaker than the ordinary countable axiom of choice.

For example, let
\mathbb{R}_{C}
denote the Cauchy real numbers and
\mathbb{R}
the Dedekind real numbers.
10
10

10

For constructive definitions of
\mathbb{R}_{C}
and
\mathbb{R}
, see e.g.
UFP 2013
or
Johnstone 2002
.
At the moment, by the “Cauchy reals” we mean a simple quotient of the set of Cauchy sequences or approximations; in
section

8.2
we will show that under suitable axioms, these coincide with the fancier Cauchy reals of
UFP 2013
.

There is an injection
i:\mathbb{R}_{C}\to\mathbb{R}
which is not constructively an isomorphism.
It is well-known that ordinary countable choice implies that it
is
an isomorphism, i.e. that every Dedekind real is a Cauchy real.
But with our restricted
6.4
(or
Axiom LEM
), all we can get is the following.

Theorem
{AC
N
or LEM}
6.32
.

Any
crisp
Dedekind real
x::\mathbb{R}
is a Cauchy real.

Proof.

By
UFP 2013
, a Dedekind real
x:\mathbb{R}
is a Cauchy real iff there exists
c:\mathchoice{\prod_{q,r:\mathbb{Q}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}(q<r)\to(q<x)+(x<r)
.

First suppose
Axiom LEM
.
Then since
x
is crisp, and
\mathbb{Q}
and
(q<r)
are discrete, for any
q,r
with
q<r
we have
(q<x)+\neg(q<x)
.
This implies
(q<x)+(x<r)
as in
UFP 2013
.

Now suppose
6.4
.
Then since
x
is crisp, so is the family
P::S\to\mathsf{Type}
defined in
UFP 2013
as

P(q,r)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{\sum_{b:\mathbf{2}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}{\sum_{(b:\mathbf{2})}}}(b={0_{\mathbf{2}}}\to q<x)\land(b={1_{\mathbf{2}}}\to x<r),

where
S\vcentcolon\equiv\{(q,r):\mathbb{Q}\times\mathbb{Q}\mid q<r\}
is equivalent to
\mathbb{N}
.
Thus, we can get a choice function and extract
c
in the same way.
∎

Corollary
{AC
N
or LEM}
6.33
.

The induced maps
\flat\mathbb{R}_{C}\to\flat\mathbb{R}
and
\sharp\mathbb{R}_{C}\to\sharp\mathbb{R}
are equivalences.

Proof.

By
6.22
, the two statements are equivalent, so it suffices to prove the second.
By
section

3.2
, it will suffice to show
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\mathbb{R}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\prod_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}\sharp\mathsf{fib}_{i}(x)
.
But by
6.27
, this follows from
6.32
.
∎

In other words,
\mathbb{R}_{C}
and
\mathbb{R}
have the same points, but perhaps different topologies.
It is natural to wonder what those topologies are!
We will return to this in
sections

8
and
11
.

Another important question to ask is what their points
are
.
From the perspective of the cohesive part of the type theory, this is a meaningless question: their points are, by definition, the real numbers (Cauchy or Dedekind, respectively).
However, recall from
section

3
that we can also construct “the set of real numbers” entirely in the codiscrete world, yielding the type
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
shown in
fig.

4
and reproduced in
fig.

11
.
Thus, we can ask whether the set of
points
of the
space
of real numbers
\mathbb{R}

is the same as this “codiscrete set of real numbers”, i.e. is “the space of real numbers”
\mathbb{R}
really a “topology” on the set of real numbers?
The answer is yes; this internalizes
Johnstone 2002
.

Theorem 6.34
.

\flat\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\simeq\flat\mathbb{R}
, and hence
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}\simeq\sharp\mathbb{R}
.

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\vcentcolon\equiv\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}}{\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(q<^{\prime}r)\times L(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U(q)\times(q<^{\prime}r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(L(q)\times U(q))\to\sharp\emptyset\right)\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(q<^{\prime}r)\rightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\sharp(L(q)+U(r))\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}L^{\prime}(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U^{\prime}(r)\right\|\mathclose{}

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}(q^{\sharp}<^{\prime}r)\times L^{\prime}(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}U^{\prime}(q)\times(q<^{\prime}r^{\sharp})\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}(L(q)\times U(q))\to\sharp\emptyset\right)\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}(q<r)\rightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\sharp(L(q)+U(r))\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}{\sum_{(L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})}}

\displaystyle\qquad\sharp\left(\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp(q<r)\times L(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}U(q)\times\sharp(q<r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}(L(q)\times U(q))\to\sharp\emptyset\right)\times\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}(q<r)\rightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|(L(q)+U(r))\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\flat\mathbb{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}}{\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}{\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}{\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}\mathsf{let}\;L^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{let}\;U^{\flat}\coloneqq N\;\mathsf{in}\;

\displaystyle\qquad\flat\sharp\left(\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp L(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp L(q)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp(q<r)\times\sharp L(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp U(r)\leftrightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}\sharp U(q)\times\sharp(q<r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}(\sharp L(q)\times\sharp U(q))\to\sharp\emptyset\right)\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}(q<r)\rightarrow\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|(\sharp L(q)+\sharp U(r))\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}}{\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}{\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}{\sum_{(M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp}))}}\mathsf{let}\;L^{\flat}\coloneqq M\;\mathsf{in}\;\mathsf{let}\;U^{\flat}\coloneqq N\;\mathsf{in}\;

\displaystyle\qquad\flat\left(\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}L(q)\right\|\mathclose{}\times\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}U(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}\flat\left(L(q)\leftrightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}(q<r)\times L(r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(r:\mathbb{Q})}}\flat\left(U(r)\leftrightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\sum_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}U(q)\times(q<r)\right\|\mathclose{}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}\flat\big((L(q)\times U(q))\to\emptyset\big)\right)\times\flat\left(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q,r:\mathbb{Q})}}\flat\big((q<r)\rightarrow\mathopen{}\left\|(L(q)+U(r))\right\|\mathclose{}\big)\right)

Figure 11.
The Dedekind reals and their codiscrete version

Proof.

First of all, notice that since
\mathsf{CodiscProp}
is codiscrete, we have

(\sharp\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})\simeq(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp}).

Thus, in
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
we can sum over
L,U:\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp}
instead; we write
L^{\prime},U^{\prime}
for their unique extensions to
\sharp\mathbb{Q}
.

Next, by the dependent version of
3.6
, the
\mathchoice{\prod_{q:\sharp\mathbb{Q}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\sharp\mathbb{Q})}}}
’s in
\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
can be replaced by
\mathchoice{\prod_{q:\mathbb{Q}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}{\prod_{(q:\mathbb{Q})}}}
, with
q
replaced by
q^{\sharp}
inside.
Now we can use the facts that
L^{\prime}(q^{\sharp})=L(q)
,
U^{\prime}(q^{\sharp})=U(q)
, and
(q^{\sharp}<^{\prime}r^{\sharp})=\sharp(q<r)
(since the inequalities on
\sharp\mathbb{Q}
extend those of
\mathbb{Q}
), and since
\sharp(q<r)
is the domain of a function with codiscrete codomain, we can replace it by
(q<r)
.
Thus we obtain the second version shown in
fig.

11
.

Now note that for
q:\sharp\mathbb{Q}
we have

L^{\prime}(q)=\sharp L^{\prime}(q)=\sharp L^{\prime}(q_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp})=\sharp L(q_{\sharp})

and similarly for
R^{\prime}
.
Likewise, for
q:\sharp\mathbb{Q}
and
r:\mathbb{Q}
we have

(q<^{\prime}r^{\sharp})=\sharp(q<^{\prime}r^{\sharp})=\sharp(q_{\sharp}{}^{\sharp}<^{\prime}r^{\sharp})=\sharp(q_{\sharp}<r).

Thus, using
section

3
(and, in the second and third lines, the fact that
\sharp
preserves products), together with the fact that
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\sharp A\right\|\mathclose{}\simeq\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}
(since both are a reflection into codiscrete propositions) we obtain the third version.

Now, since
\mathbb{Q}
is crisply discrete, we have

\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{CodiscProp})\simeq\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\flat\mathsf{CodiscProp})\simeq\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp})

using
section

6.3
in the second step.
Thus, with
section

6.1
we can convert
\flat\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
into a sum over
M,N:\flat(\mathbb{Q}\to\mathsf{DiscProp})
, obtaining the first expression for
\flat\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
in
fig.

11
.
(Since
\flat
preserves products, we have also distributed it over the
\times
s.)

However, as remarked after
section

6.3
, the equivalence
\mathsf{Disc}\simeq\flat\mathsf{Codisc}
respects all suitably-(co)reflected type formers.
Since discrete types are closed under truncations and contain
\emptyset
, we deduce the second expression for
\flat\mathbb{R}^{\prime}
in
fig.

11
.
Here we abuse notation somewhat in applying
\flat
to types that depend on cohesive variables
q,r:\mathbb{Q}
; the meaning is that since
\mathbb{Q}
is discrete, we can transfer
q,r
to elements of
\flat\mathbb{Q}
and then destruct them into crisp variables first, as in the left-hand side of (
6.4
).
Now applying
6.16
gets rid of all the
\flat
s inside the
\prod
s.
Finally, we can un-distribute the
\flat
s over the
\times
s, and apply
section

6.1
in reverse, to obtain
\flat\mathbb{R}
on the right.
∎

7.
Topos models of spatial type theory

So far we have described a
spatial type theory
with crisp variables and two modalities
\flat
and
\sharp
, and also (in
sections

2.1
,
3.1
,
3.2
and
6.4
) some classical axioms that can be added to this theory without (we expect) destroying the intended topological content.
However, nothing we have said excludes the possibility that all types are both discrete and codiscrete, with
\flat
and
\sharp
being the identity.
(In this case our classical axioms would of course apply to all types, trivializing the topology.)

Thus, in order to “do some real topology”, we need a new axiom that gives us a way to “access the topology” internally in type theory.
We could motivate such an axiom purely on first principles, but it seems appropriate at this point to instead discuss the various possible models of our theory.

So far, we have been referring mainly to classical topological spaces for intuition.
This is fine, but topological spaces do not
actually
model our theory for several reasons.
One reason is that they are not a topos, lacking universes and even a subobject classifier.
Indeed, they are not even locally cartesian closed, so do not have
\prod
-types.

Another reason is that a classical topology is “mere structure” on a set, whereas our theory demands something more.
Consider for instance the following definition.

Definition 7.1
.

A type
A
is
concrete
if
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}:A\to\sharp A
is a embedding (i.e. a
(-1)
-truncated map).

In classical topological spaces, the map from a space to its codiscrete reflection is always injective, so that all spaces would be concrete in this sense.
However, for us this is impossible.

Theorem 7.2
.

If
\mathsf{Prop}
is concrete, then all propositions are codiscrete.

Proof.

Suppose
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}:\mathsf{Prop}\to\sharp\mathsf{Prop}
is an embedding.
Then for any
P,Q:\mathsf{Prop}
, we have
(P=Q)\simeq(P^{\sharp}=Q^{\sharp})
; but the latter is
\sharp(P=Q)
by
3.7
.
Thus,
P=Q
is codiscrete.
But taking
Q=\mathbf{1}
, we have
(P=1)=P
, so all propositions are codiscrete.
∎

From a topological point of view, “all propositions are codiscrete” would mean that all continuous injections are subspace embeddings.
This seems quite dubious, and in fact it trivializes almost the entire theory:

Theorem 7.3
.

For any
n:\mathbb{N}
, an
n
-type
A
is codiscrete if and only if the map
\mathsf{const}:A\to(P\to A)
is an equivalence for all propositions
P
such that
\sharp P
.
In particular, therefore, if all propositions are codiscrete, then all
n
-types are codiscrete.

Proof.

The “only if” direction follows immediately from the universal property of
\sharp
(
3.6
), since if
P:\mathsf{Prop}
and
\sharp P
, then
(\sharp P\to A)\simeq A
.
For the “if” direction, we induct on
n
.
When
n=-1
, let
A:\mathsf{Prop}
and suppose
\mathsf{const}:A\to(P\to A)
is an equivalence for all
P:\mathsf{Prop}
such that
\sharp P
.
To show
A
is codiscrete, we assume
\sharp A
and try to prove
A
.
But then
A
is a valid choice of
P
, and of course
A\to A
; hence
A
.

Before embarking on the inductive step, we note that by
3.7
,
A\to\sharp A
is an embedding (i.e.
A
is concrete) if and only if
x=y
is codiscrete for all
x,y:A
.
On the other hand, for
x,y:A
and
P:\mathsf{Prop}
, we have

(\mathsf{const}_{x}=\mathsf{const}_{y})\simeq(P\to(x=y)).

Thus
\mathsf{const}:A\to(P\to A)
is an embedding if and only if

\mathsf{const}:(x=y)\to(P\to(x=y))

is an equivalence.
Therefore, if
A
is an
(n+1)
-type and
\mathsf{const}:A\to(P\to A)
is an equivalence for all
P:\mathsf{Prop}
such that
\sharp P
, then the inductive hypothesis ensures that
A\to\sharp A
is an embedding; thus it remains to show that it is surjective.

Let
z:\sharp A
, and write
\eta\vcentcolon\equiv(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}:A\to\sharp A
.
Then
\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)
is a proposition.
Moreover, since
\sharp
is left exact, we have
\sharp\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)\simeq\mathsf{fib}_{\sharp\eta}(z^{\sharp})
.
But
\sharp\eta
is an equivalence, so the latter type is contractible.
Thus,
\sharp\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)
, so by assumption
\mathsf{const}:A\to(\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)\to A)
is an equivalence.
But we have
\mathsf{pr}_{1}:\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)\to A
, so there is an
x:A
such that
\mathsf{pr}_{1}=\mathsf{const}_{x}
.

We claim
\eta(x)=z
.
Since this is a codiscrete goal, and we have
\sharp\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)
, by
\sharp
-induction we may assume that
\mathsf{fib}_{\eta}(z)
, i.e. there is a
y:A
and
p:\eta(y)=z
.
But
\mathsf{pr}_{1}=\mathsf{const}_{x}
yields
y=x
, and hence
\eta(x)=z
as desired.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

7.3
is a general fact about left exact monadic modalities, which generalizes the fact that a subtopos of a 1-topos is determined by its action on monomorphisms
(see e.g.
Johnstone 2002
, A4.3.6)
.
A subtopos of an
(\infty,1)
-topos, however, is not completely determined by its action on
n
-types for finite
n

(see
Lurie 2009
, §6.5)
.

Therefore, we are forced to think of our “topologies” as some more contentful “stuff” rather than just structure.
A good example to think about is the
topological topos
of
Johnstone 1979
.
Its objects are sets
X
equipped with, for every sequence
x:\mathbb{N}\to X
and point
y\in X
, a collection of “witnesses that
(x_{n})
converges to
y
”, equipped with natural operations (e.g. there is a specified “reflexivity” witness that a constant sequence converges to the point at which it is constant, etc.)
Such an object is codiscrete just when every sequence converges
uniquely
to every point.
Thus, since monomorphisms must be injective on points and witnesses both, such an object is concrete just when there is
at most one witness
that any sequence converges to any point.
But in general, a “space” in this topos can have a sequence that converges to one point in many different ways.

The topological topos is a 1-topos rather than a higher topos, so we can expect it to model all of our type theory except for full univalence.
Pending a solution to the general problem of higher-topos-theoretic semantics, we may expect that there should be a corresponding “topological
(\infty,1)
-topos” that models the whole theory with univalence.

More generally, the spatial type theory of
sections

2
to
6
should admit models in any
local topos
.
A geometric morphism
f:\mathcal{F}\to\mathcal{E}
is called
local

(see e.g.
Johnstone 2002
, §C3.6)
if its direct image
f_{*}
admits a further
\mathcal{E}
-indexed right adjoint
f^{!}
, which is then necessarily fully faithful (and so is
f^{*}
).
In this case
\mathcal{F}
inherits a comonad
f^{*}f_{*}
and a monad
f^{!}f_{*}
which are adjoint, and ought to extend the internal type theory of
\mathcal{F}
to model our
\flat
and
\sharp
(without univalence).
The restricted classical axioms of
sections

2.1
,
3.1
,
3.2
and
6.4
will hold if their ordinary versions hold in
\mathcal{E}
.
Moreover, if we enhance
f
to a local morphism of
(\infty,1)
-toposes, then it should be possible to model homotopy type theory with (at least weak) univalent universes.

Remark 7.5
.

At present, the previous paragraph is only conjectural.
What needs to be done to make it precise is (1) define an appropriate sort of “category with families” or “contextual category” to handle our two-context type theory with crisp variables and formulate
\sharp
and
\flat
as algebraic structure on such a gadget, (2) prove that syntax yields an initial one of these, and (3) construct such algebraic objects from local geometric morphisms.
Of these, (1) should be straightforward, (2) is still only conjectural even for most ordinary type theories
(the one complete theorem along these lines is in
Streicher 1991
)
, while (3) should be straightforward in the 1-topos case and, I hope, possible in the
\infty
-case, at least if we only ask for weak universes.
However, none of this is technically necessary for our actual results, which are simply theorems in our formal system; the categorical semantics is for motivation, for relative consistency, and, eventually, for applications to classical mathematics.

As shown by
Johnstone 2002
, local toposes are obtained as sheaves on sites with terminal objects admitting no nontrivial covers (“local sites”), and this was generalized to
(\infty,1)
-toposes by
Schreiber 2013
.
The topological topos arises in this way: its site has two objects
1
and
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
.
More generally, we can consider sheaves on any small full subcategory of topological spaces that contains the one-point space.
Any such topos will model spatial type theory with our classical axioms, and if we generalize to higher sheaves, it should have at least weak univalent universes.

Remark 7.6
.

A number of further examples of local geometric morphisms of 1-toposes can be found in
Johnstone 2002
, all of which generalize appropriately to
(\infty,1)
-toposes.
A “purely
\infty
-categorical” example is the
(\infty,1)
-topos of parametrized spectra, whose objects are pairs
(X,E)
where
X
is a space and
E
a spectrum parametrized over
X
; this is equivalently the category of 1-excisive functors from pointed spaces to unpointed spaces studied in Goodwille calculus
(
Goodwillie 2003
;
Lurie 2014
;
Joyal 2008
)
.
Here the discrete and codiscrete objects coincide, being those for which
E=0
, and moreover we have
\sharp=\flat
.
Finally, the (co)discrete objects are also exactly the
hypercomplete
ones, and hence include all
n
-types for finite
n
.

In particular, therefore, our “spatial type theory” is in fact significantly more general than the name suggests.
In the rest of this paper we will add axioms that bring it closer to our intended intuition that “types have topology”; studying other classes of models would of course lead us to different axioms.

Remark 7.7
.

A different sort of internal logic for local geometric morphisms (along with another class of examples arising from realizability) is considered by
Awodey & Birkedal 2003
and
Awodey et al. 1999
.
It is a “logic over a type theory” in which the types all belong to the base category but the propositions to the local topos, and there are two propositional modalities.
This theory can be roughly identified with the fragment of spatial type theory in which we allow arbitrary crisp contexts (of sets, i.e. 0-truncated types), but require all types in the cohesive context to be propositions.
(Since dependence on a proposition is always trivial, we can then assume that our cohesive propositions depend only on the crisp context, as is usual for logic-enriched type theories.)
Unfortunately, the meaning of
\flat
and
\sharp
in
ibid.
is reversed from ours; but as we will see in
section

9
there is a good reason for our choice.

As remarked in
ibid.
, when restricted to propositions, our
\flat
(their
\sharp
) satisfies the formal properties of the operator
\Box
in S4 modal logic.
Roughly speaking, this is the eventual origin of our term
modality
for
\flat
and
\sharp
(and, later on,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
).

One further remark is that the operations relating types to propositions in the logic of
ibid.
are not exactly those of spatial type theory.
On one hand, since
\prod
and
\sum
cannot be applied to crisp dependence directly, we have to wrap them in a
\flat
first to yield the quantifiers
\exists
and
\forall
of
ibid.

Specifically, if
u::A\mid\cdot\vdash P:\mathsf{Prop}
, then by “
\forall u,P
” we can only mean “
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:\flat A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}{\prod_{(x:\flat A)}}}\mathsf{let}\;u^{\flat}\coloneqq x\;\mathsf{in}\;P
”, and similarly for
\exists
.

On the other hand, if
A
is a set and
u::A
and
v::A
, we
can
form the proposition
u=v
, but this is not what is meant by “
u=v
” in
ibid.
: since that is discrete, it must instead be
\flat(u=v)
(or equivalently, by
6.1
,
(u^{\flat}=v^{\flat})
).
That this has the correct universal property
(i.e. satisfies “Lawvere’s law” from
Lawvere 1970
)
can be seen from crisp Id-induction (
5.6
), which implies that if
x::A,y::A\mid\cdot\vdash Q:\mathsf{Prop}
and
u::A\mid\cdot\vdash Q[u/x,u/y]\;\mathsf{true}
, then
x::A,y::A,p::x=y\mid\cdot\vdash Q\;\mathsf{true}
, and hence
x::A,y::A\mid q:\flat(x=y)\vdash Q\;\mathsf{true}
by
\flat
-induction.

Returning to our goal of “accessing the topology” internally in type theory, one natural approach would be to pick one local topos with a “topological” character, or a class of them, and look for special features of that model that are visible internally.
For Johnstone’s topological topos, this approach has been pursued by
Escardo & Streicher 2016
, in the following way.
One can define internally the
generic convergent sequence

\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
to be the type of non-increasing functions
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
; in the topological topos this does in fact yield the space
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
.
Thus, one can define a
convergent sequence
in an arbitrary type
X
to be a map
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}\to X
.
If we wanted to combine this approach with spatial type theory, we could add axioms ensuring that the discrete and/or codiscrete types, as defined using
\flat
and
\sharp
, can be characterized in terms of
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
.
11
11

11

Note that the notion of “indiscrete” referred to in the title of
Escardo & Streicher 2016
is weaker than our “codiscrete”; the former asks only that every sequence converges to every point in
some
way.

7.2
and
7.3
imply that the universe cannot be codiscrete in our stronger sense without largely trivializing the theory.

This is an interesting direction for future research, but in this paper we take a different route, corresponding to a different choice of a small full subcategory of topological spaces.
We are interested in applications to theorems about “manifold-like” spaces locally modeled on the real numbers, both because of their intrinsic interest, because homotopy types presented by such spaces play an important role in classical algebraic topology, and because of their importance in applications (e.g. to physics).
While manifold-like spaces are generally well-enough behaved that their topology is detected by convergent sequences, so that they embed fully faithfully into the topological topos, the generating object
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
of this topos is
not
at all manifold-like.
In particular, it is not even
locally connected
, a property that is important for ensuring that a space has a well-behaved fundamental
\infty
-groupoid.

Thus, our motivating model will instead be the topos of sheaves (or the higher topos of higher sheaves) on the full subcategory of topological spaces whose objects are the
cartesian spaces

\mathbb{R}^{n}
.
This higher topos was studied by
Schreiber 2013
under the name
Euclidean-topological cohesion
, its objects being called
Euclidean-topological
\infty
-groupoids
or
continuous
\infty
-groupoids
; in other places they are called
topological stacks
or
topological
\infty
-stacks
.
In the same way that the topological topos defines “topology” to consist of convergent sequences, this topos defines “topology” to consist of
continuous paths
(and, more generally, homotopies and higher homotopies between such paths).
In particular, just as the former allows spaces in which a sequence converges to a limit in many ways, our topos allows paths that ‘‘are continuous’’ in many ways; the ‘‘concrete objects’’ will be those in which any path is continuous in at most one way.
12
12

12

In general, when interpreted in a local topos of sheaves on a local site, our definition of “concrete” reduces to the notion of “concrete sheaf” studied by
Dubuc 1979
;
Dubuc & Espa“˜nol 2006
;
Baez & Hoffnung 2011
.
Its formulation using
\sharp
was pointed out by Carchedi and appears in
Schreiber 2013
.

We emphasize again that these “paths” are totally different from the terms of identity type (or “identifications”) that in homotopy type theory are sometimes called “paths”.
For instance, the
topological circle

\mathbb{S}^{1}
contains many continuous paths, but no identifications other than reflexivity (inside type theory, it will be a “set”), whereas the
homotopical circle

S^{1}
contains
\mathbb{Z}
-many identifications but no nontrivial topology (in fact, we have seen already in
6.21
that it is topologically discrete).
A general object of our (higher) topos is a sort of stack, with
both
topological paths and nontrivial identifications, and moreover it can have “topology on its identifications”.
A good example is the delooping of a topological group such as
O(n)
; this is homotopically a 1-type, but its loop type is
O(n)
with its topological structure.

Just as
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
plays a central role in the internal theory of the topological topos, we should expect the space of real numbers
\mathbb{R}
to play an analogously central role for us.
We would like to be able to
define
this space internally, just as
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
can be defined internally using decreasing binary sequences.
As remarked in
section

6.4
, there are at least two obvious ways to define “the real numbers” internally, so we should inquire whether either of these definitions automatically inherits the correct topology.

The answer is that the
Dedekind
real numbers do inherit the correct topology, whereas the Cauchy real numbers do not.
In fact, the Cauchy real numbers are
discrete
; we will prove this internally in
8.26
.
The correctness of the topology on the Dedekind reals requires a more involved semantic argument (see
Lin 2014
and
section

8.3
), but in
11.5
we will prove a corresponding internal approximation.
Both of these proofs use the axiom of
real-cohesion
to be introduced in the second part of the paper, which enhances our spatial type theory by asserting that the “topology” of types is determined by maps out of the Dedekind real numbers.

Remark 7.8
.

Before continuing, let us note that having moved away from the idea of types as classical topological spaces, we also need to update our subsidiary intuitions.
For example, we noted before that monomorphisms of topological spaces are continuous injections; but in topological toposes the monomorphisms must be injective not only on points but on “topological structure” (e.g. witnesses to convergence of a sequence or continuity of a path).
Such a monomorphism is a subspace inclusion when it is also “full” on topological structure (e.g. for any path lying in the subspace, the witnesses of its continuity in the subspace map isomorphically to the witnesses in the ambient space).

The case of (regular) epimorphisms (corresponding to type-theoretic surjections) is somewhat more subtle and involves the Grothendieck topology with which we equip our site.
In Johnstone’s topological topos, a map
f:A\to B
is epimorphic if it is surjective on points and any (witness of a) convergent sequence
(b_{n})\to b_{\infty}
in
B
has a “subsequence”
(b_{n_{k}})\to b_{\infty}
that is the image of some (witness of a) convergent sequence in
A
.
More importantly for us, in our topos of Euclidean-topological spaces,
f:A\to B
is epimorphic if for any continuous “plot”
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to B
, we can cover
\mathbb{R}^{n}
by open balls such that the restriction of our plot to any such ball is in the image of some plot in
A
.
If we think of
A
and
B
as a sort of manifold locally modeled on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, then the epimorphisms are the continuous maps that admit local sections.

8.
Cohesion and real-cohesion

Recall that
\mathbb{R}
denotes the
Dedekind
real numbers (since it is much more important for us than any other kind of real numbers, we dignify it by omitting any subscript).
We may call a map
\mathbb{R}\to A
a
(continuous) path
in
A
.
If topology is to be determined by such paths, then a
discrete
type should be one in which every such path is constant.
This is what the following axiom expresses.

Axiom R
\flat
.

A crisp type
A::\mathsf{Type}
is discrete if and only if
\mathsf{const}:A\to(\mathbb{R}\to A)
is an equivalence.

Remark 8.1
.

As stated, this “axiom” has to be formulated as an unjustified rule rather than an element of some fixed type, like our original
Axiom LEM
.
However, as we did for
Axiom LEM
in
section

6.3
, by using
\flat
we can reformulate it as an assumed (crisp) element of

\mathchoice{\prod_{B:\flat\mathsf{Type}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}{\prod_{(B:\flat\mathsf{Type})}}}\mathsf{let}\;A^{\flat}\coloneqq B\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{isdiscrete}(A)\leftrightarrow\mathsf{isequiv}(\mathsf{const}_{A,\mathbb{R}})).

8.1.
Cohesion

When
Axiom R

♭

is added to spatial type theory, we call it
real-cohesive type theory
(or real-cohesive homotopy type theory, if homotopical features such as univalence and HITs are included).
We now explore the consequences of
Axiom R

♭

in stages, by introducing a sequence of weaker axioms that use successively more properties of
\mathbb{R}
.
In addition to clarifying the exposition, this makes it clear that many results are true much more generally.
By
cohesive (homotopy) type theory
we mean spatial type theory with one or more of these weaker axioms.

We begin with the following, the weakest of the axioms of cohesion.

Axiom C0
.

There is a type family
R::I\to\mathsf{Type}
such that a crisp type
A::\mathsf{Type}
is discrete if and only if
\mathsf{const}:A\to(R_{i}\to A)
is an equivalence for all
i:I
.

Since this characterization of discreteness is purely internal, we finally have a notion of “discrete” that applies also to non-crisp types.

Definition
{C0}
8.2
.

An arbitrary type
A:\mathsf{Type}
(not necessarily crisp) is said to be
discrete
if
\mathsf{const}:A\to(R_{i}\to A)
is an equivalence for all
i:I
.

Lemma
{C0}
8.3
.

Discrete types are an exponential ideal, and even a “dependent exponential ideal”: if
B:A\to\mathsf{Type}
and each
B(x)
is discrete, then so is
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}B(x)
.

Proof.

\left(R_{i}\to\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}B(x)\right)\simeq\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}(R_{i}\to B(x))\right)\simeq\left(\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}B(x)\right)
.
∎

Technically we also need to verify that this equivalence is the map
\mathsf{const}
, but this is usually easy, so we omit it, both here and in the following lemmas.

Lemma
{C0}
8.4
.

Discrete types are closed under
\sum
: if
A
is discrete and
B:A\to\mathsf{Type}
has each
B(x)
discrete, then
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)
is discrete.

Proof.

We have
