\left(R_{i}\to\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)\right)\simeq\left(\mathchoice{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}{\sum_{(f:R_{i}\to A)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(r:R_{i})}}}B(f(r))\right)
.
But since
A\to(R_{i}\to A)
is an equivalence, the latter is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}(R_{i}\to B(x))
, which is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}B(x)
since each
B(x)
is discrete.
∎

Lemma
{C0}
8.5
.

If
A
is discrete and
x,y:A
, then
(x=y)
is discrete.

Proof.

Since
\mathsf{const}:A\to(R_{i}\to A)
is an equivalence, it induces an equivalence on identity types:

(x=y)\simeq(\mathsf{const}_{x}=\mathsf{const}_{y}).

However,
(\mathsf{const}_{x}=\mathsf{const}_{y})
is equivalent to
R_{i}\to(x=y)
.
∎

Lemma
{C0}
8.6
.

Discrete types are closed under pullbacks.

Proof.

If
A,B,C
are discrete and
f:A\to C
,
g:B\to C
, then

A\times_{C}B=\mathchoice{\sum_{(x:C)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:C)}}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:C)}}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:C)}}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}{\sum_{(x:C)}}}\mathchoice{\sum_{(y:B)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(y:B)}}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}{\sum_{(y:B)}}}(f(x)=g(y)),

so this follows from
sections

8.1
and
8.1
.
∎

8.2.
Punctual cohesion

Our next batch of consequences uses the additional assumption that each
R_{i}
is inhabited (which is clearly true for
\mathbb{R}
).

Axiom C1
.

Axiom C0
holds, and moreover we have some
r:\mathchoice{\prod_{i:I}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}R_{i}
.

This makes
section

8.1
equivalent to a weaker-looking definition.

Lemma
{C1}
8.7
.

A type
A
is discrete if and only if every function
R_{i}\to A
is constant, i.e. for all
i:I
and
f:R_{i}\to A
there is an
a:A
such that
f(x)=a
for all
x:R_{i}
.

Proof.

The given condition says that each
\mathsf{const}:A\to(R_{i}\to A)
has a section; but it always has a retraction, namely evaluation at
r_{i}
.
Thus, having a section is equivalent to being an equivalence.
∎

The primary application of
Axiom C1
for us is the following fact.

Lemma
{C1}
8.8
.

All propositions are discrete.

Proof.

Since each
R_{i}
is inhabited, if
A
is a proposition and
R_{i}\to A
, then
A
is contractible; hence the condition of
section

8.2
holds.
∎

From the
\flat
-
\sharp
adjunction, we obtain a partial dual:

Lemma
{C1}
8.9
.

All crisp propositions are codiscrete.

Proof.

We are to show that for any
P::\mathsf{Prop}
,
P
is codiscrete, i.e. that
\sharp P\to P
holds.
Since
P
and
\sharp P
are both discrete by
section

8.2
, it will suffice to prove
\flat\sharp P\to\flat P
.
But by
6.22
,
\flat\sharp P\simeq\flat P
.
∎

For instance, this implies that we can dispense with the distinction between “crisp and discrete” and “crisply discrete”.

Lemma
{C1}
8.10
.

If
A::\mathsf{Type}
is crisp and discrete, then we may assume it is crisply discrete.

Proof.

Suppose
A::\mathsf{Type}
and that we have some
d:\mathsf{isdiscrete}(A)
.
Since
\mathsf{isdiscrete}(A)
is a proposition, it is discrete, and so we might as well have
d:\flat(\mathsf{isdiscrete}(A))
.
Thus, by
\flat
-induction, no matter our goal we are free to assume
d^{\prime}::\mathsf{isdiscrete}(A)
.
∎

Similarly, if a crisp function is an equivalence, it is automatically a crisp equivalence; if a crisp type is an
n
-type, it is crisply an
n
-type; and so on.

As another particular case of
section

8.2
, we have:

Theorem
{C1}
8.11
.

\emptyset
is codiscrete, i.e.
Axiom

⁢
♯
∅

holds.

Proof.

\emptyset
is a crisp proposition.
∎

Corollary
{LEM,C1}
8.12
.

For any proposition
P
we have
\sharp P\simeq\neg\neg P
.

Proof.

This is just
3.15
, but with
Axiom

⁢
♯
∅

replaced by
Axiom C1
, which implies it (by
8.11
).
∎

In particular, even though
Axiom C1
only characterizes discreteness explicitly, in the presence of
Axiom LEM
it implies an even more explicit characterization of codiscreteness, at least for
n
-types.

Corollary
{LEM,C1}
8.13
.

An
n
-type
A
is codiscrete if and only if
\mathsf{const}:A\to(P\to A)
is an equivalence for all propositions
P
such that
\neg\neg P
.

Proof.

Combine
sections

8.2
and
7.3
.
∎

Remark 8.14
.

In topos-theoretic language,
Axiom

⁢
♯
∅

says that the subtopos defined by
\sharp
is
dense
, while the stronger
section

8.2
says that
\sharp
is in fact
fiberwise dense
in the sense of
Johnstone 2002
.

We also obtain a characterization of discrete
sets
(i.e. 0-types) that makes no reference to the family
R
, and coincides with that of
Penon 1985
;
Dubuc & Penon 1986
: they are the sets with decidable equality.

Lemma
{LEM,C1}
8.15
.

A set
A
is discrete if and only if
\forall x,y:A.(x=y\vee x\neq y)
.

Proof.

If
A
is discrete, then given
x,y:A
we may assume they are crisp.
Then since
A
is a set,
x=y
is a crisp proposition, hence
x=y\vee x\neq y
follows from
Axiom LEM
.

Conversely, suppose
\forall x,y:A.(x=y\vee x\neq y)
.
Since
x=y
and
x\neq y
are incompatible propositions, we have
((x=y)\vee(x\neq y))\simeq((x=y)+(x\neq y))
, so we can define functions by cases on equality in
A
.
Now for any
f:R_{i}\to A
, define
g:R_{i}\to\mathbf{2}
by

g(r)=\begin{cases}{1_{\mathbf{2}}}&\quad\text{if }f(r)=f(r_{i})\\
{0_{\mathbf{2}}}&\quad\text{if }f(r)\neq f(r_{i}).\end{cases}

Since
\mathbf{2}
is discrete,
g
is constant.
But
g(r_{i})={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
, so
g(r)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
for all
r:R_{i}
, i.e.
f(r)=f(r_{i})
for all
r
.
Hence
f
is constant.
∎

We record a few more useful consequences.
Firstly,
\flat
detects emptiness:

Corollary
{C1}
8.16
.

If
A::\mathsf{Type}
and
\neg\flat A
, then
\neg A
.

Proof.

If
\neg\flat A
, then it may as well hold crisply by
section

8.2
, i.e. we have
f::\flat A\to\emptyset
.
Then by
section

6.3
, we also have
A\to\sharp\emptyset
, and hence
\neg A
since
\sharp\emptyset=\emptyset
.
∎

Next, any injective continuous function with discrete codomain also has discrete domain, as we would expect from classical topology.

Lemma
{C1}
8.17
.

Any subobject of a discrete type is discrete.

Proof.

If
A
is discrete and
P:A\to\mathsf{Prop}
is a predicate, then each
P(x)
is discrete by
section

8.2
; hence the subobject
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}P(x)
is discrete by
section

8.1
.
∎

Corollary
{C1}
8.18
.

If
m::A\to B
is a crisp embedding, then its naturality square for
\flat
is a pullback:

\textstyle{\flat A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\flat B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B}

Proof.

Since
\flat
preserves embeddings,
\flat A\to\flat B
is an embedding, as is the pullback of
A
to
\flat B
.
Thus, it will suffice to show that the latter pullback factors through
\flat A
as a subobject of
\flat B
.
But since it is a subobject of a discrete type, it is discrete, so this follows from the universal property of
\flat A
.
∎

We can also characterize the concrete objects (
section

7
) more exactly.

Corollary
{LEM,C1}
8.19
.

A set
A
is concrete if and only if it is
\neg\neg
-separated, i.e.
\neg\neg(x=y)\to(x=y)
for all
x,y:A
.

Proof.

By definition,
A
is concrete if
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})^{\sharp}:A\to\sharp A
is an embedding.
But by
3.7
,
(x^{\sharp}=y^{\sharp})\simeq\sharp(x=y)
, so this holds just when
x=y
is codiscrete for all
x,y:A
.
Now apply
section

8.2
.
∎

For example, since
\mathbb{R}
is always
\neg\neg
-separated
(see for example
Johnstone 2002
, D4.7.6)
, it follows that
\mathbb{R}
is concrete.
But in fact, this is true even without LEM.

Theorem
{C1}
8.20
.

\mathbb{R}
is concrete.

Proof.

It will suffice to prove that for any crisp
A::\mathsf{Type}
and
f,g::A\to\mathbb{R}
, if the two composites
A\to\mathbb{R}\to\sharp\mathbb{R}
are equal, then so are
f
and
g
.
(Applying this when
A=\mathbb{R}\times_{\sharp\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{R}
will then show that
\mathbb{R}\to\sharp\mathbb{R}
is monic.)
Moreover, by
section

6.3
, saying that the two composites
A\to\mathbb{R}\to\sharp\mathbb{R}
are equal is the same as saying that the two composites
\flat A\to A\to\mathbb{R}
are equal.

Now a map
f:A\to\mathbb{R}
is determined by two
\mathbb{Q}
-indexed families of subobjects of
A
, say
L^{f}(q)
and
U^{f}(q)
for
q:\mathbb{Q}
, satisfying the usual axioms, and likewise for
g
.
Thus, our assumption says that
L^{f}(q)
and
U^{f}(q)
agree with
L^{g}(q)
and
U^{g}(q)
when pulled back to
\flat A
, and we want to show that
L^{f}(q)=L^{g}(q)
and
U^{f}(q)=U^{g}(q)
already over
A
.
By
section

8.2
, the pullback of
L^{f}(q)
to
\flat A
is
\flat L^{f}(q)
, and likewise for all the others.
(Note that
\mathbb{Q}
, being abstractly isomorphic to
\mathbb{N}
, is discrete, so we can assume any rational number to be crisp).

Now if
q<r
, we have
L^{f}(q)\cap U^{f}(r)=\emptyset
, and thus
\flat L^{f}(q)\cap\flat U^{f}(r)=\emptyset
.
But
\flat U^{f}(r)=\flat U^{g}(r)
by assumption, so
\flat L^{f}(q)\cap\flat U^{g}(r)=\emptyset
as well.
Since
\flat
preserves pullbacks, by
section

8.2
we have
L^{f}(q)\cap U^{g}(r)=\emptyset
.
If further
r<s
, then since
U^{g}(r)\cup L^{g}(s)=A
, we have
L^{f}(q)\subseteq L^{g}(s)
.
Finally, for any
p:\mathbb{Q}
we have
L^{f}(p)=\bigcup_{q<p}L^{f}(q)\subseteq\bigcup_{q<p}L^{g}(p)=L^{g}(p)
(by taking
s\vcentcolon\equiv p
, and
r
between
q
and
p
).
The same argument applies in the other direction and to all the other subsets.
∎

We can also show the inclusion of the discrete retopologization is injective (indeed, bijective) on points — but with a truncation restriction.

Theorem
{C1}
8.21
.

If
B::\mathsf{Type}
is a crisp set, then
\flat B\to B
is injective.

Proof.

Note that
\flat B
is a set by
6.6
.
In the proof of
section

6.1
, we showed that for any
u,v:\flat B
we have
(u=v)\simeq\mathsf{code}(u,v)
, where

\mathsf{code}(u,v)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;y^{\flat}\coloneqq v\;\mathsf{in}\;\flat(x=y)).

However, if
B
is a set, then
x=y
is a proposition, hence discrete by
section

8.2
.
Thus, by
\flat
-induction we can prove that
\mathsf{code}(u,v)
is equivalent to

\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;(\mathsf{let}\;y^{\flat}\coloneqq v\;\mathsf{in}\;(x=y))

which by a couple of commuting conversions is equivalent to

(\mathsf{let}\;x^{\flat}\coloneqq u\;\mathsf{in}\;x)=(\mathsf{let}\;y^{\flat}\coloneqq v\;\mathsf{in}\;y)

i.e. to
(u_{\flat}=v_{\flat})
.
Thus,
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt})_{\flat}
is injective.
∎

The proof also makes clear why we should
not
expect
\flat B\to B
to be an embedding if
B
is not a set: in that case we also have to discretify the identity types.

Corollary
{C1}
8.22
.

If
B::\mathsf{Type}
is a crisp set, then the composite
\flat B\to B\to\sharp B
is injective.

Proof.

It is equal to the composite
\flat B\cong\flat\sharp B\to\sharp B
, and
\sharp B
is also a crisp set.
∎

Corollary
{C1}
8.23
.

Any crisp discrete set is concrete.∎

We can also show that discrete
sets
are closed under surjective quotients
(see also
Johnstone 2002
, A4.6.6)
.

Lemma
{C1}
8.24
.

If
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
are crisp,
A
is discrete,
B
is a set, and
f::A\to B
is surjective, then
B
is discrete.

Proof.

The kernel-pair of
f
is a subobject of
A\times A
, and is therefore crisply discrete by
section

8.2
.
But
B
is the set-coequalizer of that kernel pair, hence discrete by
6.21
.
∎

Corollary
{C1}
8.25
.

If
A
is crisp and discrete, then so is
\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}
.∎

Now we can prove the following, which was claimed in
section

7
to hold in our topos model.

Theorem
{C1}
8.26
.

The Cauchy real numbers
\mathbb{R}_{C}
are discrete.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{Q}
and
\mathbb{N}
are discrete, by
section

8.1
, the type
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Q}
of sequences of rational numbers is discrete.
The type
\mathcal{C}
of Cauchy sequences is a subobject of
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbb{Q}
, so by
section

8.2
it is also discrete.
Finally, the Cauchy reals are a surjective image of the set of Cauchy sequences, so by
section

8.2
they are also discrete.
∎

8.26
is about the usual sort of Cauchy real numbers defined as a quotient of the set of Cauchy sequences.

UFP 2013
constructs a “better” set of Cauchy real numbers that is constructively Cauchy complete.
However, our classicality axioms suffice to ensure that the usual
\mathbb{R}_{C}
is already Cauchy complete, hence coincides with that of
UFP 2013
:

Corollary
{AC,C1}
8.27
.

\mathbb{R}_{C}
is Cauchy-complete.

Sketch of proof.

Since
\mathbb{R}_{C}
is discrete by
8.26
and the quotient map
\mathcal{C}\to\mathbb{R}_{C}
is surjective, by
6.30
there exists a section of it.
Thus, for any Cauchy sequence in
\mathbb{R}_{C}
there exists a sequence of sequences of rational numbers, and “diagonalizing” this in the usual way we obtain a limit in
\mathbb{R}_{C}
.
∎

Finally, we can also nail down the exact relationship between the topology on the two types of real numbers.

Corollary
{AC
N
or LEM, C1}
8.28
.

\mathbb{R}_{C}\simeq\flat\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

By
section

6.4
,
\flat\mathbb{R}_{C}\simeq\flat\mathbb{R}
.
But
\mathbb{R}_{C}
is discrete, so
\flat\mathbb{R}_{C}\simeq\mathbb{R}_{C}
.
∎

We will return to the question of what the topology on
\mathbb{R}
itself is in
section

11
.

8.3.
Omniscience principles

We have seen that the ordinary LEM and AC are inconsistent with spatial interpretations, motivating the introduction of
\sharp
and
\flat
in order to state our modified
Axiom LEM
and
Axiom AC
.
However, there are weaker “classicality” principles whose ordinary versions
are
true in our motivating model, and some of them can even be proven from our current axioms, such as the following.

Theorem
{LEM,C1}
8.29
.

The
limited principle of omniscience (LPO)
holds: for any
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
, either there exists an
n:\mathbb{N}
such that
f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
, or
f(n)={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
for all
n
.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
is discrete by
section

8.1
, we may assume
f::\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
is crisp.
Then “
\exists n.f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
” is a crisp proposition, so by
Axiom LEM
we have either
\exists n.f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
or
\neg(\exists n.f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}})
.
But
\neg(\exists n.f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}})
is the same as
(\forall n.f(n)={0_{\mathbf{2}}})
, so we are done.
∎

In particular, this implies that if we define
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
as in
Escardo & Streicher 2016
to be the type of non-increasing binary sequences
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
, then the canonical map
\mathbb{N}+\mathbf{1}\to\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
is an equivalence.
In particular,
every
type in our theory is “indiscrete” in the sense of
Escardo & Streicher 2016
.
Thus, the “topology” of the types in cohesive type theory is very different from the “topology” studied by
Escardo & Streicher 2016
.

It is well-known in constructive mathematics that LPO implies the following weaker classicality principles.

Corollary
{LEM,C1}
8.30
.

The
lesser limited principle of omniscience (LLPO)
holds: for any
f,g:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
, if it is not the case that both
\exists n.f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
and
\exists n.g(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
, then either
\forall n.f(n)={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
or
\forall n.g(n)={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
.∎

Corollary
{LEM,C1}
8.31
.

Markov’s principle (MP)
holds: if
f:\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
and
\neg\forall n.f(n)={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
, then
\exists n.f(n)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
.∎

It is also well-known that LPO, LLPO, and MP are equivalent to statements about the order and equality of
Cauchy
real numbers.
Specifically:

(1)

LPO is equivalent to saying that
\mathbb{R}_{C}
has decidable equality.
(Thus,
8.29
and
8.2
give an alternative proof of
8.26
.)

(2)

LLPO is equivalent to
\forall x:\mathbb{R}_{C}.(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)
, hence that
\leq
is a total order on
\mathbb{R}_{C}
.

(3)

MP is equivalent to
\forall x:\mathbb{R}_{C}.(\neg(x\leq 0)\to x>0)
.

Traditionally in constructive mathematics, two real numbers
x,y
are said to be
apart
, written
x\mathbin{\#}y
, if
|x-y|>0
.
We have
\neg(x\mathbin{\#}y)\leftrightarrow(x=y)
, but in general,
x\mathbin{\#}y
is stronger than
\neg(x=y)
.
However, since
x=y
is equivalent to
|x-y|\leq 0
, Markov’s Principle implies that
x\mathbin{\#}y
and
\neg(x=y)
coincide for
Cauchy
reals.

Of course, we are generally more interested in the Dedekind reals
\mathbb{R}
than the Cauchy ones
\mathbb{R}_{C}
.
Toby Bartels has suggested the following terminology:

(1)

The
analytic LPO
claims that
\mathbb{R}
has decidable equality.

(2)

The
analytic LLPO
claims that
\forall x:\mathbb{R}.(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)
.

(3)

The
analytic MP
claims that
\forall x:\mathbb{R}.(\neg(x\leq 0)\to x>0)
.

Since (assuming
Axiom R

♭

)
\mathbb{R}
is not discrete (its identity map is not constant), the analytic LPO is false.
Somewhat more surprisingly, we will prove in
11.7
that the analytic LLPO is also false.
The analytic Markov’s principle, however, is actually
true
in our motivating model!
I conjecture that it can even be proven from
Axiom LEM
and
Axiom R

♭

, but I have not managed to show this yet.
Thus, we assume it as an additional axiom.
Actually, I prefer the following axiom, which is equivalent in the presence of
Axiom LEM
.

Axiom T
.

For any
x:\mathbb{R}
, the proposition
x>0
is codiscrete.

Theorem
{LEM,C1,T}
8.32

(Analytic Markov’s Principle)
.

If
x,y:\mathbb{R}
satisfy
\neg(x=y)
, then
x\mathbin{\#}y
.
In particular, if
\neg(x\leq 0)
, then
x>0
.

Proof.

By
Axiom T
,
x\mathbin{\#}y
is codiscrete, so by
section

8.2
it is
\neg\neg
-stable.
Thus, we can prove it by contradiction.
However,
\neg(x\mathbin{\#}y)
means
x=y
, which contradicts the assumption of
\neg(x=y)
.
∎

Axiom T
is arguably the most mysterious part of the theory.
Topologically, it says that the open subset
(0,\infty)
of
\mathbb{R}
is a sub
space
, i.e. has the induced topology.
In other words, it relates the intrinsic “topology” of
\mathbb{R}
, arising from its definition as a type, to the internal
ordering
relation defined on it.
Another way to look at it is that it ensures that the intrinsic “topology” of
\mathbb{R}
makes it into a
topological field
, i.e. that the reciprocal is continuous on the
subspace
of invertible elements.

Some considerations of models may also help to understand
Axiom T
.

Example 8.33
.

Axiom T
holds in our motivating model of sheaves on the category
\mathcal{C}
of cartesian spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

Sketch of proof.

This requires recalling a bit about the standard proof that
\mathbb{R}
inherits the correct topology in this model, which goes as follows
(see
Mac˜Lane & Moerdijk 1994
, §VI.9;
Lin 2014
)
.
For any space
X\in\mathcal{C}
, we have
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{C})/X\simeq\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{C}/X)
, which admits a local geometric morphism to
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
.
Since local geometric morphisms are orthogonal to grouplike morphisms
(
Johnstone 2002
, C3.6.10)
, and the space of real numbers is a grouplike locale, to give a real number in
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{C})/X
is equivalent to giving a real number in
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
, or equivalently a map
X\to\mathbb{R}
.
Thus, the sections of the Dedekind real number object over
X
are the continuous maps
X\to\mathbb{R}
.

Repeating this argument with
\mathbb{R}
replaced by the space of
positive
real numbers, which is also grouplike, we find that the object of positive reals in
\mathrm{Sh}(\mathcal{C})
is the sheaf of positive real-valued functions.
To say that this is a codiscrete subobject of the Dedekind reals is to say that a continuous real-valued function factors through the space of positive real numbers just when it is positive at every point, which is obviously true.
∎

The preceding argument depends crucially on the classicality of the base topos, and in particular the fact that the locale of formal real numbers (which classifies internal Dedekind real numbers) is spatial.
Indeed, we have:

Example 8.34
.

If we start from an arbitrary base topos, and construct a similar topos of sheaves on the category of cartesian
locales

R_{f}^{n}
(where
R_{f}
denotes the locale of formal reals), then
Axiom T
can fail.

Sketch of proof.

I am indebted to Bas Spitters for this argument.
There exist toposes (particularly, recursive ones such as the effective topos) in which there are uniformly continuous functions
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)>0
for all
x
but
f
does not have uniform lower bounds on all finite intervals.
Now since the metric space
\mathbb{R}
is “locally compact” in the sense of Bishop, by
Palmgren 2007
,
f
extends to a locale morphism
\bar{f}:R_{f}\to R_{f}
, and since
\mathbb{R}
is the space of points of
R_{f}
, we still have
\bar{f}(x)>0
for all
x
.
However,
\bar{f}
does not factor through the locale of formal positive reals, since by
Palmgren 2007a
if it did then it would have local uniform lower bounds.
Thus, we can repeat the proof of
section

8.3
until the last sentence, at which point we find that the “obvious” fact is now false.
∎

Note that the topos of
section

8.3
is local over its base and satisfies
Axiom R

♭

, by the same argument as before.
Thus, (modulo
section

7
)
Axiom T
does not follow from
Axiom R

♭

alone; but I do not know a countermodel to it that satisfies both
Axiom R

♭

and
Axiom LEM
.

9.
Shape

Codiscrete types are defined by the fact that functions
into
them need not be continuous; while discrete types are defined by the fact that functions
out
of them need not be continuous.
In a sense, this means that all the
non-tautological
information about codiscrete types is carried by the maps
out
of them, and likewise the non-trivial information about discrete types is carried by the maps
into
them.
Let us leave the first for another day
13
13

13

But see
10.2
and
10.3
.
and concentrate on maps
A\to B
, where
B
is discrete.

In the world of classical topological spaces, if
B
is discrete, then (at least when
A
is well-behaved, which at the moment means “locally connected”) continuous maps
A\to B
are the same as functions
\pi_{0}(A)\to B
, where
\pi_{0}(A)
is the set of connected components of
A
.
In other words,
\pi_{0}
is a
reflection
into the subcategory of discrete spaces.
Since
\pi_{0}(A)
is also the “first layer” of the homotopy type, or fundamental
\infty
-groupoid, of
A
, this suggests that mapping into discrete spaces can carry information about the latter construction.

In fact, it turns out that the single generalization from sets to
\infty
-groupoids ensures that mapping into discrete types carries
all
the information about the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid.
At least in good situations, such as our
\infty
-topos of sheaves on the category of cartesian spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid can be
defined
as a left adjoint to the inclusion of discrete spaces
(see
Schreiber 2013
, Proposition 4.3.32;
Carchedi 2015
, §3)
.
With
Axiom R

♭

in our theory, we can get an inkling of how this works internally by considering the topological circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Definition 9.1
.

The
topological circle

\mathbb{S}^{1}
is the (homotopy) coequalizer of the pair of maps

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 6.68056pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-6.68056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{R}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 13.03612pt\raise 8.27582pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.43056pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.68056pt\raise 2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 10.61804pt\raise-8.51749pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{+1}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.68056pt\raise-2.84526pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 30.68056pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathbb{R}}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces.

We might write this as
\mathbb{S}^{1}\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
.
In
9.19
we will compare this definition to some other possible definitions of the topological circle.
For now let us simply observe that it is at least
one
reasonable definition — with one caveat, namely that
\mathbb{S}^{1}
ought to be a set (i.e. have no higher identifications), and with this definition it is not obvious that this is so.
We could, of course, 0-truncate it, but that would defeat the purpose of what we are about to do, and fortunately it turns out to be unnecessary:

Theorem
{UA}
9.2
.

Suppose
R
is a set and
f:R\simeq R
an equivalence such that for all
r:R
and
m,n:\mathbb{Z}
, if
f^{n}(r)=f^{m}(r)
, then
m=n
.
(In other words, the induced action of
\mathbb{Z}
on
R
is free.)
Then the homotopy coequalizer of
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{id}}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{R}
is also a set.

Proof.

Recall that
S^{1}
denotes the
homotopical
circle, a HIT with two constructors
\mathsf{base}:S^{1}
and
\mathsf{loop}:\mathsf{base}=\mathsf{base}
.
We define
P:S^{1}\to\mathsf{Type}
by
P(\mathsf{base})\vcentcolon\equiv R
and
P(\mathsf{loop})=f
(modulo univalence).
Note that
S^{1}
is the homotopy coequalizer of
\mathbf{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbf{1}
.
Thus, by the flattening lemma
(
UFP 2013
, §6.12)
, the desired homotopy coequalizer of
\mathsf{id}_{R}
and
f
is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\sum_{x:S^{1}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}{\sum_{(x:S^{1})}}}P(x)
; so it will suffice to prove that the latter is a set.

Let
x,y:S^{1}
and
u:P(x)
,
v:P(y)
; we must prove that
(x,u)=(y,v)
is a proposition.
Now
(x,u)=(y,v)
is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\sum_{p:x=y}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:x=y)}}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:x=y)}}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(p:x=y)}}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}{\sum_{(p:x=y)}}}(p_{*}u=v)
.
Thus, suppose
p,q:x=y
and
r:p_{*}u=v
and
s:q_{*}u=v
; we must show
(p,r)=(q,s)
.
Since
r
and
s
are equalities in a set, it suffices to show
p=q
.
But this is also an equality in a set (since
S^{1}
is a 1-type), hence a proposition; thus, since
S^{1}
is connected, we may assume
x=\mathsf{base}
and
y=\mathsf{base}
.

Now under the isomorphism
(\mathsf{base}=\mathsf{base})\simeq\mathbb{Z}
, our
p
and
q
get identified with
n,m:\mathbb{Z}
respectively.
Moreover, by definition of
P
, transporting along
p
or
q
gets identified with application of
f^{n}
or
f^{m}
respectively.
Thus, from
r
and
s
we obtain
p_{*}u=q_{*}u
and hence
f^{n}(u)=f^{m}(u)
, with
u:R
, and so the freeness assumption gives
n=m
as needed.
∎

Remark 9.3
.

It should be possible to prove
9.2
using univalence only for propositions
(e.g. with
UFP 2013
, Theorem 7.2.2)
.
But the preceding proof is easier.

Corollary
{UA}
9.4
.

The topological circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a set.

Proof.

Since
\mathbb{R}
is an abelian group,
(+1):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is an equivalence.
The freeness condition in
9.2
is equivalent to saying that the unique ring homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}\to\mathbb{R}
is injective, which just says that
\mathbb{R}
has characteristic 0.
∎

The following proof contains the crucial idea involved in the adjoint characterization of fundamental
\infty
-groupoids.

Theorem
{R
\flat
}
9.5
.

For any discrete type
X
, we have
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\to X)\simeq(S^{1}\to X)
.

Proof.

The universal property of
S^{1}
means that
(S^{1}\to X)
is equivalent to

(9.1)

\mathchoice{\sum_{x:X}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:X)}}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}{\sum_{(x:X)}}}(x=x).

But by the universal property of a coequalizer,
(\mathbb{S}^{1}\to X)
is equivalent to

(9.2)

\mathchoice{\sum_{g:\mathbb{R}\to X}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}(g\circ(+1)=g).

Now
g\circ(+1)=g
is an equality in the type
\mathbb{R}\to X
, which is equivalent to
X
since
X
is discrete.
The equivalence can be implemented by evaluating at any
r:\mathbb{R}
, such as
0
; thus (
9.2
) is equivalent to

(9.3)

\mathchoice{\sum_{g:\mathbb{R}\to X}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}{\sum_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to X)}}}(g(1)=g(0)).

But since
(\mathbb{R}\to X)\simeq X
, we may assume
g
is constant at some
x:X
, giving (
9.1
).
∎

In particular, the identity map of
S^{1}
corresponds to a nontrivial map
\mathbb{S}^{1}\to S^{1}
, which may be said informally to “wrap the topological circle around the homotopical one”.
Since
9.5
is easily shown to be natural, this map exhibits
S^{1}
as a
reflection
of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
into the discrete types.
(Recall from
6.21
that
S^{1}
is
topologically
discrete.)

Since
S^{1}
is, or should be, the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, this leads us to ask: does every type have a reflection into the discrete types?
The answer is yes, and it requires only the much weaker
Axiom C0
.

Definition
{C0}
9.6
.

For any type
A
, its
shape

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is the higher inductive type with the following five constructors.

(1)

\sigma_{A}:A\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A

(2)

\kappa_{A}:\mathchoice{\prod_{i:I}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}(R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A

(3)

For all
i:I
and
g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
and
x:R_{i}
, an equality
g(x)=\kappa_{A}(i,g)
.

(4)

\kappa^{\prime}_{A}:\mathchoice{\prod_{i:I}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}(R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A

(5)

For all
i:I
and
x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
, an equality
x=\kappa^{\prime}_{A}(i,\mathsf{const}_{x})
.

Remark 9.7
.

We use the word “shape” not only because it is nine syllables shorter than “fundamental
\infty
-groupoid”, but because the latter has various connotations that we want to be free of.
In particular, the shape is a purely internal construction in our “real-cohesive type theory”, although it generally
behaves like
the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid.
Moreover, the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid is usually notated
\Pi_{\infty}
, but since
\Pi
is rather overworked in type theory already, a different symbol is preferable.
The word “shape” comes from
shape theory
, which also studies generalizations of the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid that make sense for less well-behaved spaces.
The symbol
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
is not an integral sign (
\int
) but an “esh”, the IPA sign for a voiceless postalveolar fricative (English
sh
).

The definition of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is cooked up precisely to admit a map from
A
and to be discrete:

Lemma
{C0}
9.8
.

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is discrete.

Proof.

The last four constructors say exactly that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to(R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)
has both a left and a right inverse.
∎

It is unsurprising, therefore, that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
has the desired universal property.
We express this first as a
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
-induction
principle.

Theorem
{C0}
9.9
.

If
P:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to\mathsf{Type}
is a family of discrete types, and we have
d:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(\sigma(x))
, then we have
f:\mathchoice{\prod_{y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}P(y)
such that
f(\sigma(x))=d(x)
for all
x:A
.

Proof.

The “basic” induction principle of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
arising from its higher inductive definition says that given
Q:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to\mathsf{Type}
together with

(1)

d_{\sigma}:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}Q(\sigma(x))

(2)

d_{\kappa}:\mathchoice{\prod_{(i:I)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char (3)

d_{=}:\mathchoice{\prod_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char , where

is the third constructor of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
,

(4)

d_{\kappa^{\prime}}:\mathchoice{\prod_{(i:I)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char
