\prod_{(h:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:R_{i}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}Q(g(x)))}}{\prod_{(h:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:R_{i}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}Q(g(x)))}}}Q(\kappa^{\prime}(i,g))

(5)

d_{=^{\prime}}:\mathchoice{\prod_{(i:I)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(i:I)}}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}{\prod_{(i:I)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}\mathchoice{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}{\prod_{(y:Q(x))}}}(y=_{\zeta}d_{\kappa^{\prime}}(i,\mathsf{const}_{x},\mathsf{const}_{y}))
, where
\zeta
is the last constructor of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
,

we have
f:\mathchoice{\prod_{y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}P(y)
such that
f(\sigma(x))\equiv d_{\sigma}(x)
(plus four other equalities).
Thus, given our current hypotheses, it remains to construct
d_{\kappa}
,
d_{=}
,
d_{\kappa^{\prime}}
, and
d_{=^{\prime}}
.

First suppose
{g:R_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A}
and
{h:\mathchoice{\prod_{x:R_{i}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}{\prod_{(x:R_{i})}}}Q(g(x))}
, for some
i
.
By the third constructor of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
, we have
g(x)=\kappa(i,g)
for all
x:R_{i}
.
Thus, transporting
h(x)
along these equalities, we get
h^{\prime}:R_{i}\to Q(\kappa(i,g))
.
Thus, since
Q(\kappa(i,g))
is discrete, there is a
d_{\kappa}(i,g,h):Q(\kappa(i,))
such that
d_{=}(i,g,h,x):h^{\prime}(x)=d_{\kappa}(i,g,h)
for all
x:\mathbb{R}
, as desired.

Now suppose
x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
and
y:Q(x)
.
By the last constructor of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
, we have
x=\kappa(i,\mathsf{const}_{x})
.
Thus, transporting
y
along this equality, we obtain
y^{\prime}:Q(\kappa(i,\mathsf{const}_{x}))
.
Since
Q(\kappa(i,\mathsf{const}_{x}))
is discrete, we have
d_{=^{\prime}}(i,x,y):y^{\prime}=d_{\kappa^{\prime}}(i,\mathsf{const}_{x},\mathsf{const}_{y})
as desired.
∎

Corollary
{C0}
9.10
.

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}R_{i}
is contractible for any
i:I
.

Proof.

It is inhabited by
\kappa_{R_{i}}(i,\sigma_{R_{i}})
, so it remains to show
x=\kappa_{R_{i}}(i,\sigma_{R_{i}})
for all
x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}R
.
Since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}R_{i}
is discrete, so is this equality type; so by
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
-induction, it suffices to prove
\sigma_{R_{i}}(x)=\kappa_{R_{i}}(i,\sigma_{R_{i}})
for all
x:R_{i}
.
But this follows from the third constructor of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}R_{i}
.
∎

Corollary
{R
\flat
}
9.11
.

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{R}
is contractible.∎

Corollary
{C1}
9.12
.

For any
A
, the map
\sigma:A\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is surjective.
In particular, if
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\right\|\mathclose{}
then
\mathopen{}\left\|A\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Proof.

Given
y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
, define
P(y)\vcentcolon\equiv\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\sum_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:A)}}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}{\sum_{(x:A)}}}\sigma(x)=y\right\|\mathclose{}
.
By
section

8.2
, each
P(y)
is discrete.
Thus, by
9.9
, to prove
\mathchoice{\prod_{y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}{\prod_{(y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)}}}P(y)
(i.e. that
\sigma
is surjective), it suffices to prove
\mathchoice{\prod_{x:A}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(x:A)}}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}{\prod_{(x:A)}}}P(\sigma(x))
; but this is obvious.
∎

Remark 9.13
.

Recall from
section

4
that many positive type formers have a “crisp” variant of their induction principle, so that we can do case analysis on a
u::A+B
or induction on a
u::\mathbb{N}
.
This is
not
the case for
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
, however.
If it were, then we could do
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
-induction on
u::\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
, so that if
B
were discrete then any
M:B
depending on
u::A
would factor through
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
.
Specializing to
A\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbb{R}
and
B\vcentcolon\equiv\mathbf{2}
, any
M:\mathbf{2}
depending on
u::\mathbb{R}
would be constant.
However, since
u
is crisp, by using the crisp LEM we can define
M:\mathbf{2}
to be
{0_{\mathbf{2}}}
if
u<0
and
{1_{\mathbf{2}}}
otherwise, which is not at all constant.

Now we have the reflection property:

Theorem
{C0}
9.14
.

For any
A:\mathsf{Type}
and any discrete
B:\mathsf{Type}
, composition with
\sigma:A\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
induces an equivalence

(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to B)\simeq(A\to B).

Proof.

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
-induction into the constant family
{\lambda}x.\,B
yields a section of
(\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\circ\sigma)
.
Thus, it suffices to show that given
h,k:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to B
, if
h\circ\sigma=k\circ\sigma
, then
h=k
.
This follows from another
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
-induction with
P(x)\vcentcolon\equiv(h(x)=k(x))
, which is discrete by
section

8.1
.
∎

Moreover, analogously to how
\flat\dashv\sharp
crisply (
section

6.3
), we have
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\dashv\flat
crisply.

Theorem
{C0}
9.15
.

For any
A,B::\mathsf{Type}
, there is a natural equivalence

\flat(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to B)\simeq\flat(A\to\flat B).

Proof.

Since
\flat B
is discrete, by
9.14
we have a natural equivalence

(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to\flat B)\simeq(A\to\flat B)

and this equivalence is preserved by
\flat
.
On the other hand, since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is discrete, by
section

6.2
we have a natural equivalence

\flat(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to\flat B)\simeq\flat(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to B).

Composing these two equivalences yields the conclusion.
∎

9.14
makes
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
into a monadic modality, like
\sharp
, and
its construction as a localization makes it an
accessible
one
(see
Rijke et al. 2016
)
.
Unlike
\sharp
, however,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
is not left exact.
To show this, we introduce our final weakening of
Axiom R

♭

.

Axiom C2
.

Axiom C1
holds, and moreover there exist
i_{0}:I
and
r_{0},r_{1}:R_{i_{0}}
such that
R_{i_{0}}
is a set and
r_{0}\neq r_{1}
.

Note that
Axiom R

♭

implies
Axiom C2
, since
0\neq 1
in
\mathbb{R}
.

Theorem
{C2}
9.16
.

There exists a pullback that is not preserved by
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
.

Proof.

The pullback of the inclusions
r_{0},r_{1}:\mathbf{1}\to R_{i_{0}}
is
\emptyset
.
But
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbf{1}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}R_{i_{0}}
are both contractible, whereas
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\emptyset=\emptyset
since it is already discrete.
∎

(However, like any monadic modality,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
does preserve products.)

Viewing
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
as a reflection gives another, more category-theoretic, way to phrase and prove
9.5
.

Theorem
{R
\flat
}
9.17
.

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}=S^{1}
.

Proof.

By
9.14
,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
is a left adjoint to the inclusion of discrete types in all types.
Thus, it preserves all colimits, which is to say it takes colimits in the category of all types to colimits in the category of discrete types.
Thus, since
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is the (homotopy) coequalizer of
\mathbb{R}\rightrightarrows\mathbb{R}
, it follows that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}
is the coequalizer
in the category of discrete types
of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{R}\rightrightarrows\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{R}
, and hence by
section

9
of
\mathbf{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbf{1}
.
Now
S^{1}
is the coequalizer of
\mathbf{1}\rightrightarrows\mathbf{1}
in the category of
all
types; but by
6.21
it lies in the subcategory of discrete types and hence is also the coequalizer there.
∎

Similar methods may be used in many other examples.
That is, given a “cell complex” presentation of a classical topological space, if we can convert it into both a specification for a HIT and a colimit decomposition of that space that is sufficiently “cofibrant”, then
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
will preserve that colimit and take the space to the HIT.
We do have to be careful to avoid the sort of cell complex used in classical algebraic topology where discs are glued along their
boundaries
, because such gluing in a constructive world tends to produce inhomogeneous “cusps”.
For instance, identifying the two endpoints of the topological interval
[0,1]=\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid 0\leq x\leq 1\}
would not
14
14

14

Although, amusingly, its shape would still be
S^{1}
: it would be the coequalizer of
1\rightrightarrows[0,1]
, while
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}[0,1]
is also contractible since
[0,1]
is a retract of
\mathbb{R}
.
produce a space equivalent to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
This is also a known “defect” (if one regards it so) of the topos model we have in mind; see also the remarks after
11.7
.
(It is “fixed” in the topological topos of
Johnstone 1979
, but in that model no left adjoint
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
can exist due to a lack of local connectedness.)

However, in many cases it should be possible to remedy this by gluing along
open overlaps
instead.
What makes this work is the fact that although
\mathbb{R}
fails to satisfy the trichotomy principle constructively, we do have
(x<z)\to(x<y)\lor(y<z)
for all
x,y,z:\mathbb{R}
.
Combined with the following observation, this tells us that gluing along open overlaps produces no cusps.

Lemma 9.18
.

If
f:A\to C
and
g:B\to C
are embeddings, then their union as sub-types of
C
is their pushout under their intersection:
A\cup B\simeq A\sqcup^{A\cap B}B
.

Proof.

By definition,
f\cup g:A\cup B\to C
is the smallest embedding through which both
f
and
g
factor, while
A\cap B
is the pullback
A\times_{C}B
.
If
h:D\to C
is an embedding through which both
f
and
g
factor, then the square

\textstyle{A\cap B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{B\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{A\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{D}

commutes since
h
is an embedding, so there is an induced map
A\sqcup^{A\cap B}B\to D
.
It remains to note that
A\sqcup^{A\cap B}B\to C
is an embedding by
Rijke 2017
.
∎

As an example, we give two other definitions of
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Theorem 9.19
.

The following types are equivalent.

(1)

\mathbb{S}^{1}
as defined in
section

9
, i.e. the coequalizer of the identity and
+1:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

The subset
\{(x,y)\mid x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}
of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
(the boundary of the topological disc
\mathbb{D}^{2}
).

(3)

The coequalizer of the inclusion of open intervals
(0,\varepsilon)\to(0,1)
and the translated inclusion
({\lambda}x.\,1-\varepsilon+x):(0,\varepsilon)\to(0,1)
, for any
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
.

Proof.

We can define the functions
\sin
and
\cos
on the Dedekind reals constructively, and prove that
\sin^{2}x+\cos^{2}x=1
.
Thus,
{\lambda}x.\,(\cos(2\pi x),\sin(2\pi x))
defines a map from
\mathbb{R}
to
\{(x,y)\mid x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}
, which by the periodicity of
\sin
and
\cos
descends to
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
It is then straightforward to verify that this map is both surjective and injective, hence an equivalence since both types are sets.
Thus
(1)
and
(2)
are equivalent.

Now let
0<\varepsilon<\frac{1}{2}
.
Every real number is either
<\varepsilon
or
>-\varepsilon
, so the set
\{(x,y)\mid x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}
in
(2)
is the union of its subsets
U
and
V
consisting of the points where
y<\varepsilon
and where
y>-\varepsilon
.
Thus, by
section

9
it is the pushout of
U
and
V
, each of which is isomorphic to an open interval, under their intersection, which is isomorphic to the disjoint union of two open intervals, included into
U
and
V
at their ends.
Since (again by
section

9
) the pushout of two open intervals under their intersection is their union, we can rearrange this pushout to become
(3)
.
∎

Similarly, we can obtain the topological sphere
\mathbb{S}^{2}
by gluing two topological discs
\mathbb{D}^{2}
(isomorphic to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, hence with contractible shape) along a strip
\mathbb{S}^{1}\times\mathbb{R}
(with shape
S^{1}
).
Thus
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{2}
is the homotopical suspension of
S^{1}
, i.e. the homotopical sphere
S^{2}
.
We can proceed inductively for higher spheres, and so on.
In this way it may be possible to access homotopy types that would be quite difficult to present as HITs (due, for example, to their having infinitely many constructors), by first constructing their topological versions as sets using the Dedekind reals and then applying
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
.

Remark 9.20
.

The overall conclusions drawn from
Axiom C0
in this section and
section

8
can be summarized as “
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
is a reflector into the same subcategory that
\flat
coreflects into, and it preserves finite products”.
As with the analogous conclusions about
\sharp
and
\flat
noted in
sections

3
and
6.3
, in
Schreiber & Shulman 2012
we assumed this axiomatically, while here we have deduced it from
Axiom C0
and a higher inductive definition of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
.
This difference from
Schreiber & Shulman 2012
is roughly orthogonal to our introduction of
\flat
and
\sharp
using crisp variables; it is not necessitated by anything, but it is a convenient starting point for expressing the stronger
Axiom C1
,
Axiom C2
, and
Axiom R

♭

.
These axioms were not considered in
Schreiber & Shulman 2012
; but in
section

10
we will relate the first two to corresponding categorical properties studied by
Lawvere 2007
;
Johnstone 2011
;
Lawvere & Menni 2015
.

Remark 9.21
.

The crisp adjointness
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\dashv\flat
from
9.15
finally allows us to justify the choice of the notations
\flat
and
\sharp
(the notation and justification are both due to
Schreiber 2013
).
Suppose
G
is a group, meaning a set (0-type) with a group structure (but, like all types in spatial type theory, also possessing intrinsic topology).
We can construct a delooping
\mathbf{B}G
(a.k.a.
K(G,1)
) by the methods of
Licata & Finster 2014
.

Now if
X
is any space, a
principal
G
-bundle over
X
with a flat connection
assigns to every (topological) path in
X
an element of
G
(“transport” along that path), such that concatenating paths corresponds to multiplication in
G
, and so forth.
Since paths in
X
and elements of
G
are the 1-morphisms in the fundamental
\infty
-groupoid of
X
and in
\mathbf{B}G
, respectively, such a bundle with flat connection can be described categorically as a map
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}X\to\mathbf{B}G
.
But by
9.15
, this is equivalent to a map
X\to\flat\mathbf{B}G
.
Thus,
\flat\mathbf{B}G
is the
moduli space of flat
G
-connections
, justifying the notation
\flat
.
The notation
\sharp
is chosen simply as dual to flat; although one can argue that if
Z
classifies bundles of some sort then
\sharp Z
classifies analogous bundles that are “sharp” in that their fibers can vary discontinuously.
(Unfortunately, as noted in
section

7
, in
Awodey & Birkedal 2003
and
Awodey et al. 1999
the symbols
\flat
and
\sharp
were used for the propositional restriction of the same operations, but with reversed meanings.)

10.
Axioms of cohesion

The axioms introduced in
sections

8
and
9
form a linear string of implications:

R
\flat

\Rightarrow
C2
\Rightarrow
C1
\Rightarrow
C0.

Our main goal (the Brouwer fixed point theorem) requires
Axiom R

♭

(or at least its consequence
section

9
).
However, the weaker versions are much more general assumptions, which are not very specific to the “topological” situation and are satisfied in many other models, so it is interesting that they suffice for most of the general theory.
In this somewhat digressive section, we investigate some of their further consequences and their topos-theoretic meaning.

10.1.
Axiom C0
means stable local connectedness

Combining
sections

6.3
and
9.15
, we see that
Axiom C0
gives us a crisp “adjoint triple”
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\dashv\flat\dashv\sharp
, in which
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
preserves finite products.
In topos-theoretic language, the category of types is “local and locally connected” over the category of discrete (or, equivalently, codiscrete) types, and in addition the left adjoint preserves binary products.

Johnstone 2011
called this additional condition
stable local connectedness
.

Conversely, if a topos is local and stably locally connected, then by combining
Johnstone 2002
with
Johnstone 2011
, it has a site of definition that is local and locally connected and closed under finite products.
We should therefore be able to show that
Axiom C0
holds in its internal spatial type theory by taking
I
to be the discrete set of objects of the site and
R_{i}
the corresponding representable.
The condition that
A\to(R_{i}\to A)
be an equivalence then says, in terms of sheaves, that
A(U)\to A(R_{i}\times U)
is an isomorphism (or, in the higher-topos case, an equivalence) for any object
U
of the site (here we use the fact that the site has finite products).
The special case
U=1
tells us that
A
is a constant (pre)sheaf, and the rest of the conditions follow automatically.
(We could also take
I
to be a set of objects that generate the site under finite products, as
\mathbb{R}
does for our site of cartesian spaces
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.)

Thus,
Axiom C0
is exactly a type-theoretic incarnation of stable local connectedness (as an additional property added to a local topos).

Schreiber 2013
calls a local and stably locally connected
\infty
-topos
cohesive
(although for
Lawvere 2007
the word “cohesive” also includes the categorical version of
Axiom C1
, below, and an additional axiom called “continuity” that we do not consider here).

In addition to our motivating example (sheaves on cartesian spaces), several other similar examples of local and stably locally connected toposes and
\infty
-toposes can be found in
Schreiber 2013
.
Here are a few more:

•

By
Johnstone 2011
and
Johnstone 2002
, the topos
\mathrm{Sh}(X)
of sheaves on a space (or locale) is local and stably locally connected if and only if
X
has both a focal point and a dense point.
For example, in the Sierpinski space, the closed point is focal and the open point is dense.

•

The local
\infty
-topos of parametrized spectra mentioned in
section

7
is stably locally connected, with
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}=\sharp
.
In fact, it is also punctually locally connected (see below).

•

Rezk 2014
shows that “global equivariant homotopy theory” forms a local and stably locally connected
\infty
-topos.

10.2.
Axiom C1
means punctual local connectedness

If we additionally assume
Axiom C1
, then by
section

8.2
the discrete objects are closed under subobjects.

Johnstone 2011
shows that this is equivalent to the following condition, there called
punctual local connectedness
(
Lawvere 2007
calls it the
Nullstellensatz
; and
Schreiber 2013
calls it
pieces have points
).

Theorem
{C1}
10.1

(
Johnstone 2011
, Lemma 2.3
)
.

For any
A::\mathsf{Type}
, the composite
\flat A\to A\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is surjective.

Proof.

As in
UFP 2013
, it suffices to show that
\mathopen{}\left\|\flat A\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}\to\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}
is surjective.
Thus, since epimorphisms of sets are surjections
(
UFP 2013
, Lemma 10.1.4)
, it suffices to show (invoking the universal property of
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\hskip 1.0pt\text{--}\hskip 1.0pt}\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}
) that
(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to C)\to(\flat A\to C)
is injective for any set
C
.
Moreover, since in the proof of Lemma 10.1.4 from
UFP 2013
we only need to instantiate this property with one particular set
C
, in fact it suffices to show that

(10.1)

\flat(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to C)\to\flat(\flat A\to C)

is injective for any
crisp
set
C
.
And by
section

6.2
, we may as well assume
C
is also discrete (otherwise we could apply
\flat
to it).

Now we can essentially copy the proof of Lemma 2.3 from
Johnstone 2011
.
Firstly, note that (
10.1
) is equal to the composite

\flat(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\to C)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\flat(A\to C)\xrightarrow{\;\flat\;}\flat(\flat A\to\flat C)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\flat(\flat A\to C)

where the first equivalence is
9.14
and the last is because
C
is discrete.
Thus, it will suffice to show that the middle map is injective.
But this map is equal to the composite

\flat(A\to C)\longrightarrow\flat(A\to\sharp C)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\flat(\flat A\to C)\xrightarrow{\simeq}\flat(\flat A\to\flat C)

where the middle equivalence is
section

6.3
and the last is because
C
is discrete.
But
C\to\sharp C
is injective by
section

8.2
.
∎

Thus,
Axiom C1
implies punctual local connectedness.
Conversely, by
Johnstone 2011
, any punctually locally connected topos has a site of definition that is local and locally connected and in which every object has a global point.
Clearly in this case if we take
I
and
R_{i}
as described above, then
Axiom C1
will hold; thus it is exactly a type-theoretic incarnation of punctual local connectedness.

In the different terminology of
Lawvere & Menni 2015
,
10.1
(along with our previous results) means that the category of types is “pre-cohesive” over that of (co)discrete types.
In fact, it is “stably pre-cohesive”, because all of our theorems admit an arbitrary crisp context.

10.3.
Axiom C2
means contractible codiscreteness

Following
Lawvere & Menni 2015
further, we investigate the shapes of codiscrete types, for which purpose we bring back
Axiom C2
(page
Axiom C2
).

Theorem
{LEM,C2}
10.2
.

If
A
is codiscrete, then
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is a proposition.

Proof.

Let
x,y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
; we must show
x=y
.
By
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
-induction, we may assume that
x=\eta(u)
and
y=\eta(v)
for
u,v:A
.
Let
i_{0}
and
r_{0},r_{1}:R_{i_{0}}
be as in
Axiom C2
.
Since they are axioms, they are crisp, and so we have
r_{0}{}^{\flat}:\flat R_{i_{0}}
and
r_{1}{}^{\flat}:\flat R_{i_{0}}
.
By
6.1
,
(r_{0}{}^{\flat}=r_{1}{}^{\flat})
is equivalent to
\flat(r_{0}=r_{1})
, i.e. to
\flat\emptyset
and hence to
\emptyset
.
In other words, we have
(r_{0}{}^{\flat}\neq r_{1}{}^{\flat})
.

Now since
\flat R_{i_{0}}
is a crisp discrete set, by the flat LEM it has decidable equality.
Thus, there is a map
f:\flat R_{i_{0}}\to A
with
f(r_{0}^{\flat})=u
and
f(r_{1}{}^{\flat})=v
.
But the inclusion
\flat R_{i_{0}}\to R_{i_{0}}
is inverted by
\flat
and hence also by
\sharp
, so since
A
is codiscrete our
f
extends to a map
g:R_{i_{0}}\to A
with
g(r_{0})=u
and
g(r_{1})=v
.
Now the composite
g\circ\eta:R_{i_{0}}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
has
g(r_{0})=x
and
g(r_{1})=y
; but as
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A
is discrete,
g
is constant, so
x=y
.
∎

Corollary
{LEM,C2}
10.3
.

For any crisp
B
we have
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\sharp B=\mathopen{}\left\|B\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Proof.

For any
B
we have
B\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\sharp B
, hence a map
\mathopen{}\left\|B\right\|\mathclose{}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\sharp B
.
But
\flat B\simeq\flat\sharp B\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\sharp B
is surjective by
10.1
, and factors through
\mathopen{}\left\|B\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Hence our map
\mathopen{}\left\|B\right\|\mathclose{}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\sharp B
is also surjective, thus an equivalence.
∎

Lawvere & Menni 2015
call the conclusion of
10.2

connected codiscreteness
, and
section

10.3
(proven there as Lemma 7.3)
explicit connected codiscreteness
.
In a homotopical context, however, it seems better to say
contractible
codiscreteness.
As before, since we can work in an arbitrary crisp context, we automatically have “stable contractible codiscreteness”.

Remark 10.4
.

Unfortunately, the phrase “
X
is contractible” is used by
Lawvere & Menni 2015
to mean “
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(Y\to X)\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}\simeq\mathbf{1}
for all
Y
”, which conflicts with its standard usage in homotopy type theory to mean “
X\simeq\mathbf{1}
”.
(They don’t mention the 0-truncation since they are working with 1-toposes.)
The intution behind their definition is that the sets
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(Y\to X)\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}
are the hom-sets of a “strong homotopy category” of types, analogous to the strong homotopy category of topological spaces obtained by identifying homotopic maps, so this definition says that
X
is terminal in that category.
Since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
is a functor from this strong homotopy category to the category of discrete spaces and their 0-truncated hom-sets, and it preserves the terminal object, if
X
is contractible in the sense of
ibid.
then
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}X\simeq\mathbf{1}
, i.e.
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}X
is contractible in the sense of homotopy type theory.
Thus, if we want to keep “
X
is contractible” to mean
X\simeq\mathbf{1}
, we could use a phrase like
strongly spatially contractible
for the “contractibility” of
Lawvere & Menni 2015
.

Conversely, if a punctually locally connected topos satisfies
10.2
, then we have an object, namely
\sharp\mathbf{2}
, for which
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\sharp\mathbf{2}
is contractible, but which has two unequal elements
{0_{\mathbf{2}}}^{\sharp}
and
{1_{\mathbf{2}}}^{\sharp}
.
(By
3.7
we have
({0_{\mathbf{2}}}^{\sharp}={1_{\mathbf{2}}}^{\sharp})\simeq\sharp({0_{\mathbf{2}}}={1_{\mathbf{2}}})\simeq\sharp\emptyset
, which is empty by
8.11
.)
Thus, we can make
Axiom C2
hold by adding
\sharp\mathbf{2}
to the collection of
R_{i}
’s; so
Axiom C2
is exactly a type-theoretic incarnation of (punctual local connectedness and) contractible codiscreteness.

We could now copy the proof of
Lawvere & Menni 2015
to show that every type
A
embeds into a type
B
such that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}B
is contractible, there called
sufficient cohesion
.
I conjecture that
Axiom R

♭

and
Axiom AC
should together also imply the property called
continuity
in
Lawvere 2007
;
Menni 2014
— that the canonical map
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(A^{S})\to(\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A)^{S}
is an equivalence for any crisp set
A
and any crisp discrete set
S
— but I have not managed to prove this yet.

Axiom R

♭

does not seem to have been studied yet in a topos-theoretic context.
Of course, it is much more restrictive even than
Axiom C2
; rather than defining a general class of toposes for investigation, it singles out an important characteristic of a single topos (or a small group of toposes) that we are already interested in.

11.
Synthetic topology

As a warm-up for Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem, in this section we develop some aspects of synthetic topology that stay in the world of sets (0-types).
Here we can already see the power of
Axiom R

♭

, even before any homotopy theory enters: it forces
\mathbb{R}
to be
connected
in several senses.
One such sense is the following.

Theorem
{R
\flat
}
11.1
.

If
P:\mathbb{R}\to\mathsf{Prop}
is a “detachable subset” (i.e. for all
x:\mathbb{R}
we have
P(x)\vee\neg P(x)
), then either
\forall x,\,P(x)
or
\forall x,\,\neg P(x)
.

Proof.

A detachable
P:\mathbb{R}\to\mathsf{Prop}
yields a function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbf{2}
, where
f(x)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
if
P(x)
and
f(x)={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
if
\neg P(x)
.
But
\mathbf{2}
is discrete, so by
Axiom R

♭

f
is constant.
∎

Put differently, if
U
and
V
are subsets of
\mathbb{R}
such that
U\cup V=\mathbb{R}
and
U\cap V=\emptyset
, then either
U=\mathbb{R}
or
V=\mathbb{R}
.
In other words,
\mathbb{R}
cannot be “broken into two pieces” nontrivially.

Taylor 2010
calls this property
compact connectedness
.
Combining this with
Axiom LEM
, and also
Axiom T
from
section

8.3
, we can prove a version of the Intermediate Value Theorem (which is a sort of one-dimensional analogue of Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem).

Theorem
{LEM,R
\flat
,T}
11.2

(Discontinuous IVT)
.

Let
f::\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a crisp function and
c::\mathbb{R}
a crisp real number.
If there exist
a,b:\mathbb{R}
such that
f(a)<c<f(b)
, then there exists an
x:\mathbb{R}
such that
f(x)=c
.

Proof.

Since the hypothesis of
a
and
b
is a crisp proposition, it is discrete.
Thus all our hypotheses may as well be crisp, so we may prove
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\mathbb{R}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}f(x)=c\right\|\mathclose{}
instead, which by
section

8.2
is equivalent to
\neg\neg{\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\mathbb{R}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}f(x)=c}
.
Thus, suppose for contradiction
\neg{\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\mathbb{R}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{R})}}}f(x)=c}
, i.e. for all
x:\mathbb{R}
we have
f(x)\neq c
.
By
8.32
, for all
x
we have
f(x)\mathbin{\#}c
, hence either
f(x)<c
or
f(x)>c
.
Let
U=\{x\mid f(x)<c\}
and
V=\{x\mid f(x)>c\}
; then
U\cup V=\mathbb{R}
and
U\cap V=\emptyset
.
By
11.1
, then, either
U=\mathbb{R}
or
V=\mathbb{R}
, which is a contradiction since
a\in U
and
b\in V
.
∎

The crispness of the hypotheses
f
and
c
means that the point
x
does not vary continuously with them.
It is well-known that a continuous choice of
x
is impossible, and that this is what prevents the classical version of IVT from holding constructively

(see, for instance,
Taylor 2010
)
.
Our technology of spatial type theory enables us to state a “discontinuous” IVT, even in a world where all types are spaces, and our axioms of classicality and real-cohesion allow us to prove it.

A more common way to constructivize the IVT is to strengthen the hypothesis (e.g. that
f
“doesn’t hover” or is “locally non-constant”) or weaken the conclusion (e.g. we only find an approximate solution).
We can also use
Axiom R

♭

to prove such a form of IVT, without the need for
Axiom LEM
or
Axiom T
.

First we show that
\mathbb{R}
is connected in a different sense.
The following proof also introduces an idea that we will use repeatedly.
Since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
is a left adjoint, it takes colimits of types to colimits in the subcategory of discrete types.
Moreover, since
crisp
discrete types are also coreflective, they are closed under colimits, so
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
preserves crisp colimits of types.
Similarly, using
section

8.2
, crisp discrete sets are also closed under set-colimits (i.e. 0-truncations of homotopy colimits).
Thus, if we write
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}A
for
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}A\right\|_{0}\mathclose{}
, then the functor
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}
reflects sets into discrete sets, so it preserves crisp set-colimits.
We can then use facts about (set-)colimits of the shapes of types to conclude things about the types themselves.

Theorem
{R
\flat
}
11.3
.

Let
U,V::\mathbb{R}\to\mathsf{Prop}
be crisp subsets of
\mathbb{R}
with
U\cup V=\mathbb{R}
.
If
\mathopen{}\left\|U\right\|\mathclose{}
and
\mathopen{}\left\|V\right\|\mathclose{}
, then also
\mathopen{}\left\|U\cap V\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Proof.

By
section

9
, the assumption ensures that
\mathbb{R}
is the pushout of
U
and
V
over
U\cap V
.
Thus, the contractible type
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{R}
is the pushout of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}V
under
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(U\cap V)
, and hence the contractible
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}\mathbb{R}
is the set-pushout of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V
under
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}(U\cap V)
.
But
\mathopen{}\left\|U\right\|\mathclose{}
and
\mathopen{}\left\|V\right\|\mathclose{}
, hence also
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U\right\|\mathclose{}
and
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V\right\|\mathclose{}
.

Now if
x:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
and
y:\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V
, their images in the contractible type
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}\mathbb{R}
must be equal.
Using the explicit construction of this set-pushout as the set-quotient of an equivalence relation of zigzags on
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U+\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V
, we see that we must in particular have
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}(U\cap V)\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Finally, since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(U\cap V)\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}(U\cap V)
is 0-connected and hence surjective, while
(U\cap V)\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(U\cap V)
is surjective by
section

9
, we also have
\mathopen{}\left\|U\cap V\right\|\mathclose{}
.
∎

Taylor 2010
calls the above property
overt connectedness
.
Note that we have only proven it for crisp subsets.

The 0-truncations in the above proof could be omitted, but if we left them out, then instead of the set-based construction of set-pushouts we would need to use the van Kampen theorem
(
UFP 2013
, §8.7)
.
The former needs only the fact that the sets form a
\Pi W
-pretopos
(
UFP 2013
, Theorem 10.1.11)
, which requires only function extensionality and propositional extensionality; whereas the latter in general requires full univalence.
Similar remarks apply to the proof of
11.5
below, but in
12.6
we will need to resort to univalence and van Kampen.
(Actually, based on intuition from classical topology, it is natural to conjecture that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U
is automatically a set for any subset
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}
, but I do not know how to prove this.)

Theorem
{R
\flat
}
11.4

(Approximate IVT)
.

Let
f::\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be a crisp function and
c::\mathbb{R}
a crisp real number.
Then for any
\varepsilon>0
, if there exist
a,b:\mathbb{R}
such that
f(a)<c<f(b)
, then there exists an
x:\mathbb{R}
such that
|f(x)-c|<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

It suffices to consider the case when
\varepsilon:\mathbb{Q}^{+}
is a positive rational number.
Then since
\mathbb{Q}^{+}
is discrete, we may assume
\varepsilon
is also crisp.
Therefore the sets
U\vcentcolon\equiv\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid f(x)<c+\varepsilon\}
and
V\vcentcolon\equiv\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid f(x)>c-\varepsilon\}
are also crisp.
We have
U\cup V=\mathbb{R}
by locatedness of Dedekind reals, while by assumption
a\in U
and
b\in V
.
Thus,
11.3
supplies the desired
x
.
∎

Note that although
11.4
eliminates classicality axioms, we still have only crisp dependence on
f
and
a
.
In fact, the approximate IVT (for continuous functions) is contructively provable without any additional assumptions; the most common proofs use countable choice or uniform continuity of
f
, but
Frank 2017
has recently given a proof avoiding both of these.
So there is no topological reason for this crispness restriction, but I do not know whether it can be removed using our current methods.

On the other hand,
11.2
and
11.4
are statements about
all
functions
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
No explicit continuity hypothesis is required, because in our synthetic world “all functions are continuous”.
In fact, with a little more work we can actually prove explicitly that all functions are continuous, thereby showing that the axioms of real-cohesion really do nail down precisely the “topology” of
\mathbb{R}
to be the intended one.

Theorem
{R
\flat
,T}
11.5
.

Every crisp function
f::\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is
\varepsilon
-
\delta
continuous at every crisp real number
a::\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We will need to assume given crisp
b_{1}<a
and
b_{2}>a
such that
f(b_{1})\mathbin{\#}f(a)
and
f(b_{2})\mathbin{\#}f(a)
.
This can always be ensured by modifying
f
without changing it near
a
.
For instance,
f(a+2)-f(a)
is either
>\frac{1}{2}
or
<1
(and this is crisp since it is a proposition); if the former we can take
b_{2}=a+2
, while if the latter we can subtract
\max(0,x-a-1)
from
f
before taking
b_{2}=a+2
.

As before, we may assume
\varepsilon::\mathbb{Q}^{+}
is a crisp positive rational number.
We may also assume
\varepsilon
is less than both
|f(b_{1})-f(a)|
and
|f(b_{2})-f(a)|
.
Define

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon/2\}

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle=\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid|f(x)-f(a)|>\varepsilon/4\}

Then
U\cup V=\mathbb{R}
, so that
\mathbb{R}
is the pushout of
U
and
V
under
U\cap V
.
Let
R_{1}=(-\infty,a]
and
R_{2}=[a,\infty)
, and
U_{i}=U\cap R_{i}
,
V_{i}=V\cap R_{i}
for
i=1,2
, so that
R_{i}=U_{i}\cup V_{i}
and
R_{i}
is the pushout of
U_{i}
and
V_{i}
under
U_{i}\cap V_{i}
.
Note that
a\in U_{i}
and
b_{i}\in V_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

Consider the fibers of the map
U\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
; these are the “spatially connected components” of
U
.
Let
w
be the image of
a
in
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
, and
W
the preimage of
w
in
U
; thus
W
is the “component of
U
containing
a
”.
Similarly, define
w_{i}
and
W_{i}
for
i=1,2
to represent the component of
U_{i}
containing
a
.
Then the function
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U_{i}\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
maps
w_{i}
to
w
, so that
W_{i}
is a subset of
W
.
Similarly, let
Z_{i}
be the component of
V_{i}
containing
b_{i}
, with image
z_{i}
in
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.

Now as remarked above,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}
prerves crisp set-colimits.
Thus, the contractible
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}R
is the set-pushout of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V
under
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}(U\cap V)
, and similarly for
i=1,2
.
In particular,
w_{i}
and
z_{i}
are identified in the set-pushout of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U_{i}
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}V_{i}
under
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}(U_{i}\cap V_{i})
, and so
w_{i}
must be equal to the image of some element of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}(U_{i}\cap V_{i})
.
Since
X\to\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}X
is surjective by
section

9
,
W_{i}
must contain an element of
U_{i}\cap V_{i}
.
Let
y_{i}
be such an element.
Since
y_{i}\in V_{i}
, we have in particular
f(y_{i})\neq f(a)
, and hence
y_{i}\neq a
.
By
8.32
,
y_{i}\mathbin{\#}a
; so since
y_{i}\in R_{i}
we have
y_{1}<a<y_{2}
.
Finally, since
y_{i}\in U_{i}
, also
y_{i}\in U
.

Let
\delta=\min(y_{2}-a,a-y_{1})
; I claim this is the
\delta
to our
\varepsilon
.
Thus, suppose
x:\mathbb{R}
and
|x-a|<\delta
, hence
x\in(y_{1},y_{2})
.
(Note that
x
is
not
crisp.)
Either
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon
or
|f(x)-f(a)|>\varepsilon/2
; the first is what we want, so it suffices to show that the second leads to a contradiction.

Thus, suppose
|f(x)-f(a)|>\varepsilon/2
.
Then since any
p\in U
satisfies
|f(p)-f(a)|<\varepsilon/2
by definition of
U
, we have
f(p)\neq f(x)
, hence
p\neq x
.
By
8.32
, therefore,
p\mathbin{\#}x
, and thus either
p<x
or
x<p
.
In other words,
\mathchoice{\prod_{p:U}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}}(p<x)\vee(x<p)
, which is equivalent to
\mathchoice{\prod_{p:U}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\prod_{(p:U)}}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}{\prod_{(p:U)}}}(p<x)+(x<p)
since
y
cannot be both greater and less than
x
.
It follows that we can define a map
q:U\to\mathbf{2}
by
q(p)={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
if
p<x
and
q(p)={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
if
x<p
.
In particular, since
y_{1}<x
and
x<y_{2}
, we have
q(y_{1})={0_{\mathbf{2}}}
and
q(y_{2})={1_{\mathbf{2}}}
.
But
y_{1}
and
y_{2}
are also both in
W
, i.e. they are identified in
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
.
Since
\mathbf{2}
is a discrete set,
q:U\to\mathbf{2}
must factor through
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}U
, and so
q(y_{1})=q(y_{2})
, a contradiction.
So it must be that the other case holds:
|f(x)-f(a)|<\varepsilon
, as desired.
∎

The statement that all functions
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
are
\varepsilon
-
\delta
continuous is sometimes called “Brouwer’s theorem”, because Brouwer proved it in his “intuitionistic mathematics” using choice sequences.
Here we have proved a version of it using instead our
Axiom R

♭

and
Axiom T
.
(Both uses of
Axiom T
could also be replaced by the assumption that
f
is “strongly extensional”, i.e.
f(x)\mathbin{\#}f(y)
implies
x\mathbin{\#}y
.)

11.5
provides an almost complete answer to the question of “what is the topology of
\mathbb{R}
”, with “topology” interpreted in the sense relevant to our intended model, namely “functions out of
\mathbb{R}
”.

Corollary
{LEM,R
\flat
,T}
11.6
.

The canonical function
\flat(\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R})\to(\flat\mathbb{R}\to\flat\mathbb{R})
is an injection, and coincides with the inclusion of the
\varepsilon
-
\delta
continuous functions.

Proof.

To show it is an injection, suppose given
f,g::\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\flat f=\flat g
as functions
\flat\mathbb{R}\to\flat\mathbb{R}
, or equivalently
\sharp f=\sharp g
as functions
\sharp\mathbb{R}\to\sharp\mathbb{R}
.
We must show that for any
x:\mathbb{R}
we have
f(x)=g(x)
; but since
\mathbb{R}
is concrete by
8.20
, it suffices to show
f(x)^{\sharp}=g(x)^{\sharp}
, which follows from
\sharp f=\sharp g
.

Now
11.5
implies that this injection lands inside the
\varepsilon
-
\delta
continuous functions.
Conversely, suppose
f:\flat\mathbb{R}\to\flat\mathbb{R}
is
\varepsilon
-
\delta
continuous.
By
Axiom LEM
applied in the universe of discrete types,
f
is locally uniformly continuous therein (meaning that it is uniformly continuous on all finite intervals).
Since it suffices to consider intervals with rational endpoints, while
\mathbb{Q}
is discrete and discrete types are closed under all logical operations,
f
is also locally uniformly continuous in the world of all types.
Therefore, so is the composite
\mathbb{Q}\to\flat\mathbb{R}\xrightarrow{f}\flat\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.
But it can be proved constructively that any locally uniformly continuous function
\mathbb{Q}\to\mathbb{R}
extends uniquely to
\mathbb{R}
; see
Troelstra & Dalen 1988
.
And such an extension restricts to
f
on
\flat\mathbb{R}
, since the two agree on
\mathbb{Q}
and both are continuous.
∎

It would be even better to characterize the “topology” of
\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
itself and so on, or equivalently the functions
\mathbb{R}^{n}\to\mathbb{R}
for all
n:\mathbb{N}
, but I don’t know how to do this without additional axioms.
However, the general method of proof of
11.5
does have various further applications.
Here we mention only one more: the promised falsity of the analytic LLPO from
section

8.3
.

Theorem
{LEM,R
\flat
}
11.7
.

It is not the case that for all
x:\mathbb{R}
we have
x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0
.

Proof.

By
section

8.2
, it suffices to show
\neg\flat(\forall x:\mathbb{R}.(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0))
.
For contradiction, suppose
\flat(\forall x:\mathbb{R}.(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0))
, hence
\forall x:\mathbb{R}.(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0)
crisply.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be such that
f(0)=0
and
f(x)=x\sin(\frac{\pi}{x})
for any
x\mathbin{\#}0
.
Such a function can be defined constructively without too much trouble, and since it needs no hypotheses it is crisp.
Let
U=\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid x\geq 0\wedge f(x)\geq 0\}
and
V=\{x:\mathbb{R}\mid x\geq 0\wedge f(x)\leq 0\}
; then
U
and
V
are also crisp.
Our assumption implies that
U\cup V=[0,\infty)
.

Classically,
U
would be the disjoint union of
\{0\}
with the intervals
[\frac{1}{n+1},\frac{1}{n}]
for even
n
, and
V
the similar union for odd
n
.
(Here and subsequently, we adopt the convention that when
n=0
, the interval
[\frac{1}{n+1},\frac{1}{n}]
means
[1,\infty)
.)
In fact,
Axiom LEM
and
Axiom C1
suffice to make this true, by the following argument.

Let
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
be the type of functions
\mathbb{N}\to\mathbf{2}
that take the value
{1_{\mathbf{2}}}
at most once (this is equivalent to the type of non-increasing sequences).
Now, for any even
n
and any
x\in U
, we have
(x<\frac{1}{n-1})\vee(x>\frac{1}{n})
; but since
x\notin(\frac{1}{n},\frac{1}{n-1})
, it follows that
(x\leq\frac{1}{n})\vee(x>\frac{1}{n})
.
Similarly,
(x\geq\frac{1}{n+1})\vee(x<\frac{1}{n+1})
, and so the proposition
x\in[\frac{1}{n+1},\frac{1}{n}]
is decidable (given that
x\in U
).
Thus, we can define
p:U\to\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
by

p(x)_{n}=\begin{cases}{1_{\mathbf{2}}}&\quad x\in[\frac{1}{2n+1},\frac{1}{2n}]\\
{0_{\mathbf{2}}}&\quad x\notin[\frac{1}{2n+1},\frac{1}{2n}].\end{cases}

If we write
\underline{n}:\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
for the characteristic sequence of
n:\mathbb{N}
, and
\infty:\mathbb{N}_{\infty}
for the constantly-
{0_{\mathbf{2}}}
sequence, then

p^{-1}(\underline{n})=[\frac{1}{2n+1},\frac{1}{2n}]
and
p^{-1}(\infty)=\{0\}
.
On the other hand, as for any function,
U\simeq\mathchoice{\sum_{n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N}_{\infty})}}}p^{-1}(n)
.
But by LPO (
8.29
),
\mathbb{N}_{\infty}=\mathbb{N}+\mathbf{1}
, so we have

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle\simeq\{0\}+\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}\textstyle[\frac{1}{2n+1},\frac{1}{2n}]

and similarly

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle\simeq\{0\}+\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}\textstyle[\frac{1}{2n+2},\frac{1}{2n+1}].

Now as remarked above,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
preserves coproducts of crisp types (such as the summands of
U
and
V
).
Similarly, for any
P:\mathbb{N}\to\mathsf{Type}
, by the generalization of
UFP 2013
, Theorem 7.3.9
to monadic modalities we have

\displaystyle\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}P(n)\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\bigl(\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}P(n)\bigr)

\displaystyle\simeq\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}P(n)

(since this is already discrete, as
\mathbb{N}
is).

Thus,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U\simeq\mathbf{1}+\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}{\sum_{(n:\mathbb{N})}}\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}[\frac{1}{2n+1},\frac{1}{2n}]
and similarly for
V
.
But any closed interval
[a,b]
is a retract of
\mathbb{R}
, hence
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}[a,b]
is a retract of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{R}
and thus contractible.
Therefore,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U\simeq\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
and also
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}V\simeq\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
.

Recalling that
[0,\infty)=U\cup V
, it follows that
[0,\infty)
is the pushout of
U
and
V
under their intersection.
However, this intersection is just
\{0\}\cup\{\frac{1}{n}\mid n:\mathbb{N}\}
, which is another copy of
\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
.
Since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
preserves this pushout, the contractible type
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}[0,\infty)
is the pushout of
\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
and
\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
under
\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
.
Moreover, the two maps
\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}\rightrightarrows\mathbf{1}+\mathbb{N}
in this pushout can be identified with the identity and with “
+1
” acting on
\mathbb{N}
, respectively (arising from the inclusions of
\frac{1}{n}
as the left endpoint of one interval and the right endpoint of another).
But the pushout of these two maps is
\mathbf{2}
rather than
\mathbf{1}
, since there is nothing to identify the elements of
\mathbf{1}
(which correspond to
\{0\}
in
[0,\infty)
) with anything else.
This is a contradiction, so our assumption
\flat(\forall x:\mathbb{R}.(x\leq 0\vee x\geq 0))
is false.
∎

11.7
explains why classical “closed” cell complexes must be avoided in real-cohesion, as mentioned in
section

9
.
Namely, the analytic LLPO claims that the evident map
(-\infty,0]+[0,\infty)\to\mathbb{R}
is surjective, which would be exactly what we need in order to be able to glue (say) the endpoints of a closed interval together seamlessly; its failure means that such a gluing will not give the desired answer.
(Conversely, therefore, if one wants a “topological topos” in which classical cell complexes
do
work, one has to give up on local connectedness, as done by
Johnstone 1979
.)

12.
The Brouwer fixed-point theorem

Finally we are ready to attack Brouwer’s fixed-point theorem.
We confine ourselves to the simplest version, which is about self-maps of the disc:

\mathbb{D}^{2}\vcentcolon\equiv\{(x,y):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid x^{2}+y^{2}\leq 1\}.

Recall from
9.19
that the boundary of the disc

\partial\mathbb{D}^{2}\vcentcolon\equiv\{(x,y):\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mid x^{2}+y^{2}=1\}

is an equivalent definition of the topological circle
\mathbb{S}^{1}
; it is the only one we will use in this section.
Recall also from
9.17
that
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}\simeq S^{1}
.
We also have:

Lemma
{R
\flat
}
12.1
.

\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{D}^{2}
is contractible.

Proof.

Since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{R}
is contractible and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
preserves finite products,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
is also contractible.
But
\mathbb{D}^{2}
is a retract of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
, by the function

(x,y)\mapsto\left(\frac{x}{\max(1,\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}})},\frac{y}{\max(1,\sqrt{x^{2}+y^{2}})}\right).

Hence
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{D}^{2}
is a retract of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
, and thus also contractible.
∎

In particular, therefore, we have:

Lemma
{UA,R
\flat
}
12.2
.

\mathbb{S}^{1}
is not a retract of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.

Proof.

If it were,
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}
would be a retract of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{D}^{2}
and hence contractible.
But
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}=S^{1}
, which is not contractible since its loop space is
\mathbb{Z}

(
Licata & Shulman 2013
)
.
∎

So much for the homotopy-theoretic part of the proof; we proceed to the topological part.
This might be done in many ways, but we choose to be explicit and calculational to make it obvious that the argument is constructive.
Here is also where the mysterious
Axiom T
from
section

8.3
reappears.

Lemma
{LEM,C1,T}
12.3
.

If there is a map
f:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{D}^{2}
with no fixed point, then
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a retract of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.

Proof.

Our assumption is that for all
z:\mathbb{D}^{2}
we have
f(z)\neq z
.
Write
z=(x,y)
and
f(z)=(u,v)
; then
f(z)\neq z
means
(x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2}\neq 0
, hence by
8.32
(using
Axiom T
)
(x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2}>0
.

Now the line through
f(z)
and
z
can be parametrized as

(12.1)

(tx+(1-t)u,ty+(1-t)v)

with
t=0
being
f(z)
and
t=1
being
z
.
We are interested in the intersections of this line with
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, which are given by solving a quadratic equation:

\displaystyle(tx+(1-t)u)^{2}+(ty+(1-t)v)^{2}

\displaystyle=1\quad\iff

\displaystyle((x-u)t+u)^{2}+((y-v)t+v)^{2}

\displaystyle=1\quad\iff

\displaystyle((x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2})t^{2}+2((x-u)u+(y-v)v)t+(u^{2}+v^{2}-1)

\displaystyle=0

The standard quadratic formula yields the solutions

\displaystyle t

\displaystyle=\frac{-B\pm\sqrt{D}}{A}

where

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=((x-u)u+(y-v)v)

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle={((x-u)u+(y-v)v)^{2}-((x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2})(u^{2}+v^{2}-1)}

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle={(x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2}}.

To ensure that this yields real solutions constructively, we need to know that
A\mathbin{\#}0
and
D\geq 0
.
As we saw above, the former follows from our assumption that
f(z)\neq z
.
For the latter, we can compute

D=(x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2}-(vx-uy)^{2}.

Now
(x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2}
is the squared length of one diagonal of the parallelogram spanned by
z
and
f(z)
regarded as vectors originating at the origin, while
(vx-uy)^{2}
is the squared area of that parallelogram.
The area of a parallelogram is at most half the product of the lengths of its diagonals; thus
D\geq 0
as long as the other diagonal of the parallelogram in question is
\leq 2
.
But this other diagonal is the vector sum
z+f(z)
, which has magnitude
\leq 2
since both
z
and
f(z)
lie in
\mathbb{D}^{2}
.

Thus we constructively have two real solutions to our quadratic equation for
t
.
We choose the one in which the
\pm
sign is
+
.
Substituting this value of
t
into the equation (
12.1
) of our line, we obtain a formula for a point in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
that is a function of
(x,y)
and
(u,v)
, hence (recalling that
(u,v)=f(x,y)
) of
z=(x,y):\mathbb{D}^{2}
only.
Moreover, by construction, this point always lies in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
.
Let us denote it
r(z)
; thus we have
r:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

It remains to show that
r
is a retraction of the obvious inclusion, i.e. that if
z=(x,y)
lies in
\mathbb{S}^{1}
, then
r(z)=z
.
For this, it suffices to show that if
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
, then our chosen value of
t
becomes equal to
1
.
This is equivalent to asking that
A+B=\sqrt{D}
, and this in turn is equivalent to asking that
A+B\geq 0
and that
(A+B)^{2}-D=0
.
For the first, we compute

\displaystyle A+B

\displaystyle=x^{2}+y^{2}-ux-vy

\displaystyle=1-(ux+vy)

But
ux+vy
is the dot product of
z
and
f(z)
, which is
\leq 1
since both have magnitude
\leq 1
.
Finally, for the second we compute

(A+B)^{2}-D=((x-u)^{2}+(y-v)^{2})(x^{2}+y^{2}-1)

which clearly vanishes if
x^{2}+y^{2}=1
.
∎

Combining
sections

12
and
12
, we see that an arbitrary
f:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{D}^{2}
cannot fail to have a fixed point.
To deduce a positive statement from this, we invoke
Axiom LEM
.

Theorem
{UA,LEM,R
\flat
,T}
12.4

(Sharp Brouwer Fixed-Point Theorem)
.

For any function
f:\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{D}^{2}
, we have
\sharp\mathopen{}\left\|\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\mathbb{D}^{2}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}f(x)=x\right\|\mathclose{}
.
That is, any self-map of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
sharply has a fixed point.

Proof.

By
section

8.2
, the claim is equivalent to
\neg\neg\mathchoice{\sum_{x:\mathbb{D}^{2}}\,}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\mathchoice{{\textstyle\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}{\sum_{(x:\mathbb{D}^{2})}}}f(x)=x
.
But this is what we have just proven.
∎

Equivalently, we can say:

Corollary
{UA,LEM,R
\flat
,T}
12.5

(Crisp Brouwer Fixed-Point Theorem)
.

For any crisp function
f::\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{D}^{2}
, there exists
x:\mathbb{D}^{2}
such that
f(x)=x
.

Proof.

If
f
is crisp, then so is the fixed point asserted by
12.4
, so we can strip the
\sharp
from it.
∎

Topologically, these formulations both say the same thing: every continuous self-map of
\mathbb{D}^{2}
has a fixed point, but such a fixed point cannot be selected continuously.
Since the existential in both theorems is propositionally truncated, even if the
\sharp
or crispness could be omitted we would not be asserting the existence of a single continuous
function
selecting fixed points.
However, according to
section

7
, we would be asserting that such a function exists
locally
, i.e. that any given function has a neighborhood of functions on which fixed points can be selected continuously; and this is just as impossible.
Thus, the versions of the theorem that we have proven really are the strongest we can expect.

On the other hand, just as we did with the Intermediate Value Theorem in
section

11
, we can eliminate
Axiom LEM
and
Axiom T
by constructing an
approximate
fixed point.
Our proof of this will also involve more synthetic homotopy theory: in addition to
\pi_{1}(S^{1})=\mathbb{Z}
, we will use the van Kampen theorem for pushouts.

Theorem
{UA,R
\flat
}
12.6

(Approximate Brouwer Fixed-Point Theorem)
.

For any crisp function
f::\mathbb{D}^{2}\to\mathbb{D}^{2}
and
\varepsilon>0
, there exists an
x:\mathbb{D}^{2}
such that
|f(x)-x|<\varepsilon
.

Proof.

As before, we may assume that
\varepsilon
is rational, and hence that it is crisp.
We may also assume
\varepsilon<1
.
Define

g(x)=\begin{cases}f(x)\cdot\frac{\min(|f(x)|,1-\varepsilon/2)}{|f(x)|}&\qquad\text{if}\;|f(x)|>1-\varepsilon\\
f(x)&\qquad\text{if}\;|f(x)|<1-\varepsilon/2\end{cases}

The case division is constructively valid by
section

9
, since one of the two cases must be true, and if both are true then the two definitions agree.
And since
\varepsilon<1
, if
|f(x)|>1-\varepsilon
then
|f(x)|>0
, so that
\frac{\min(|f(x)|,1-\varepsilon/2)}{|f(x)|}
makes sense.

Now this definition ensures that
|f(x)-g(x)|<\varepsilon/2
, so it suffices to find an
x:\mathbb{D}^{2}
such that
|g(x)-x|<\varepsilon/2
.
Moreover, we have
|g(x)-x|>\varepsilon/4
for all
x:\mathbb{S}^{1}
.

Let
U=\{x:\mathbb{D}^{2}\mid|g(x)-x|>\varepsilon/4\}
and
V=\{x:\mathbb{D}^{2}\mid|g(x)-x|<\varepsilon/2\}
.
Then
U\cup V=\mathbb{D}^{2}
, exhibiting
\mathbb{D}^{2}
as a crisp pushout of
U
and
V
under their intersection.
Therefore the contractible type
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{D}^{2}
is also the pushout of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U
and
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}V
under
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(U\cap V)
.

However,
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is contained in
U
by the construction of
g
, and the construction of the retraction
r
in
section

12
works for all
x:U
.
Thus,
\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a retract of
U
, and so
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}
is a retract of
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U
.
But since
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{S}^{1}=S^{1}
, it contains a nonidentity identification, and hence so does
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U
.
On the other hand, this identification must be mapped to the identity in the contractible type
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}\mathbb{D}^{2}
.

Now recall that the van Kampen theorem
(
UFP 2013
, §8.7)
identifies the 0-truncated equality types of a (homotopy) pushout as a certain set-quotient.
Inspecting this construction we see that the only way two identifications in
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U
can become equal in
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}U\sqcup^{\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}(U\cap V)}\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}V
is if we have
\mathopen{}\left\|\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}V\right\|\mathclose{}
.
Hence, by
section

9
, we also have
\mathopen{}\left\|V\right\|\mathclose{}
.
∎

As in
11.4
, there seems no topological reason for the crispness of
f
, but I do not know how to remove it.
Note that although the technique is similar to that in
11.3
and
11.5
, here we need to use
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}
rather than
\mathord{\raisebox{0.5pt}{\text{\rm{\char 115\relax}}}}_{0}
, and likewise we need the van Kampen theorem for pushouts that are not sets (whose proof also involves the full univalence axiom).
